#5138. Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Anthropic launched a personal finance feature for Claude on Thursday, letting users connect bank accounts and ask the chatbot what they have spent, what bills are due, and how much money they have left.

The announcement came with an accusation from crypto investor Anthony Pompliano, who says Anthropic tried to get inside information from his competing startup, Silvia, months before the rollout.

Claude can then summarize spending, spot duplicate charges or unexpected fees, explain interest payments, and build charts or bill calendars. Anthropic's example shows a user asking where their money went this month, then following up to find out what remains before payday.

"Access is read-only, and Claude can't move, manage, or invest your money," Anthropic wrote on X. "Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations," the company added.

The assistant can see balances, credit limits, account names and types, the last four digits of account numbers, and up to 24 months of transactions from accounts users approve. According to Anthropic's documentation, conversations inside the Personal Finance hub are not used to train its models, but account balances may lag and AI-generated figures should be checked against bank records.

Also, how Anthropic tracks its users’ financial data and uses it beyond model training is not completely clear—and this is extremely valuable information for big data companies.

Pompliano, whose Silvia product bills itself as a personal AI chief financial officer, posted a lengthy complaint on X shortly after Claude's announcement. He alleged that Anthropic personnel began approaching Silvia engineers in April, supposedly because they were interested in the startup's growth and computing needs.

"Anthropic was fishing for details on how we built our product and why it was so popular," he wrote. Pompliano also described an alleged steak dinner involving Anthropic representatives, claiming someone told the Silvia team that the meal was on CEO Dario Amodei's tab.

His engineers declined to divulge sensitive information, he claimed, adding that someone at Anthropic warned his team about the upcoming launch days earlier.

"Today, Anthropic launched a Claude wrapper that is masquerading as a personal finance product," Pompliano wrote. He also claimed Silvia's computing costs are roughly 97% lower when using its own models and hardware instead of frontier AI models, which most likely mean they are using a local model, likely finetuned for the startup’s specific purpose.

That said, Anthropic was not the first general-purpose chatbot maker to move into personal finance, either. OpenAI launched similar bank connections for ChatGPT in May, also using Plaid for read-only access to financial information, and Agents like Hermes, Open Claw, or Grok Bot can also do pretty similar tasks given the proper skills, permissions, and instructions.

Anthropic says Personal Finance is a first step and is exploring partnerships with licensed professionals and regulated financial firms. For now, the beta is restricted to U.S. customers on three paid Claude plans.

#5135. Gnosis Pay data breach exposes customer info; funds unaffected

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Gnosis Pay disclosed a data breach after unauthorized access to its legacy debit card system on Nov. 3. Funds were unaffected; 3D Secure is now mandatory.

Gnosis Pay disclosed a data breach affecting its legacy system powering Gnosis Pay-branded debit cards, exposing certain customer information after unauthorized access was detected on Nov. 3.

The company said it contained the incident within two hours, replaced compromised security credentials and addressed the vulnerability, adding that customer funds were not affected and its enterprise platform powering white-label card programs was untouched.

Gnosis Pay has made 3D Secure verification mandatory for online card payments until the affected system is retired on Dec. 20.

Crypto card providers increasingly rely on third-party infrastructure and legacy systems that can create security vulnerabilities. In recent months, there have been breaches exposing cardholder information and exploits affecting funds held in card accounts.

Based said an attacker accessed an internal dashboard used to manage its Visa card program on Oct. 5. Some customer data was exposed, but the attacker reportedly failed to access deposited funds. In August, a vulnerability in Rain's Solana card infrastructure allowed unauthorized withdrawals from card balances belonging to users of Tria and Avici.

Meanwhile, Revolut said an unauthorized third party used a fraudulent request from a legitimate government email domain to obtain sensitive customer information, potentially including account statements, identity documents and Bitcoin transaction histories. Customer funds and systems were unaffected.

#5134. Forget These 4 Ab Workout Myths. Train Using These Principles Instead.

Know the truth before you begin your transformation.

YOU WANT TO get in shape, and you want to look the part. So you tack on some extra time to the end of your workout to hit your abs, sure that those extra crunches will result in a six-pack sooner than later. Right?

Before we go any further, there are a handful of notions about your core training that you need to ditch immediately. If any of these sound familiar to you, don’t sweat it. You’ve likely heard about thousands of ab-building techniques, and it’s no easy feat to sift through all that information in search of the truth. What’s important is understanding what ab results are possible through consistent training. This will save you during those moments five weeks into this program when you’re questioning if you should keep going. You’ll remember what the process is, why it works, and why you really do have to log that day’s sets.

I can’t stress this enough: the abs should be treated no differently than other muscles in the body. A muscle is a muscle, and that means three things: muscles don’t work independently, muscles adapt to resistance training, and muscles need appropriate recovery to develop.

Unfortunately, when you have a topic like abs, which are arguably the most Googled and sought-after body part, you will get a large mix of opinions on how to approach training. The approach I want you to take with me as your coach is the science-based one. We aren’t going to live in the anecdotal world of “my one friend did this and got great results.” When it comes to exercise, there isn’t a one-size-fits-all solution, but there are some tried-and-true facts we have learned over the years with science and research. These are the key principles I share with my clients when it comes to breaking down the misinformation about abs.

I AM NOT sure when this myth started, but for some reason people are under the impression that abs are uniquely different from other muscles in that you can train them every day. That is absolutely not true.

Overuse of any muscle actually leads to atrophy (deterioration) of the muscle, which is the exact opposite of what you want when you are trying to build your abs. When it comes to core training and core conditioning, it’s more effective to have a mix of higher and lower intensity days, as you’ll see later in the program.

Going all out is an outdated approach to training. You simply can’t go full throttle every day and expect to stay on the ball throughout the program. There are a handful of people in the world who can do just that, and that’s amazing. But we can’t create a workout plan based on outliers. It needs to be based on general outcomes. So, in order to give yourself appropriate recovery time, challenge your muscles in different ways, and ensure continued success, it’s best to navigate between different intensity days.

THERE IS NO such thing as spot reduction.

Direct abdominal work isn’t going to burn off that layer of fat laying on top of your abs the way you might hope. Burning fat is much better attacked through nutrition, strength training, and cardiovascular exercise. The best approach is a holistic one, like I’ve designed for you in this plan.

The way you develop your core in this program will change the way you look at training moving forward. By mixing up exercises and taking a step-by-step approach to building up a strong core, you’ll see changes in everything from the way you look to the way you feel to the way you move through your day! These 90 days will build you up from the inside out into the strongest version of yourself.

BELIEVE IT OR not, they can be the same. That is the basis of this program—to blend ability with visibility.

You shouldn’t feel doomed to endless days of crunches wondering why the results just aren’t coming to you. Variety and emphasis will be large determining factors of your training. With the right mix, you will be able to get the look you want with the function you need.

HAVE YOU EVER noticed two people with differently shaped abs? Maybe one person’s abs are deeper, more symmetrical, and more defined than his buddy’s. I mean, Arnold Schwarzenegger is famous for having four visible abs no matter how shredded he got. Why is that?

Well, that is something you are born with. Thick cords of fibrous tissue stretch across the abdominal wall. The main connective tissue that runs down the center of the abdomen is called the linea alba. That line can be straight or slightly angled, depending on your genetics, and determines how symmetrical your abs appear to be. How many of the crossing tendinous fibers you have (which can be three or more) determines what kind of pack you can get, be it a six-pack, eight-pack, or even a 10-pack.

David Otey, CSCS is a fitness writer, NYC-based strength coach, and Men's Health Advisory Board member who specializes in strength and hypertrophy protocols as well as athletic performance. For more on Otey check out www.oteyfitness.com.

#5132. Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

"NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here," the project's official account tweeted.

NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here. $ZEC pic.twitter.com/rPaSFe9h7j

"This is a huge milestone for Shielded Labs," Jason McGee of Shielded Labs, the Zcash development organization behind the mechanism, tweeted ahead of the activation.

The NSM tackles a problem Zcash inherited from Bitcoin: whether, as block subsidies decline with each halving, transaction fees alone will be enough to pay miners to secure the network. Under the mechanism, 60% of transaction fees will be removed from circulation and gradually reissued through future block rewards, while preserving the 21 million ZEC supply cap. Reissuance will begin in 2031, with the recycled funds spread along a smooth curve on top of the existing halving schedule.

The NSM was Shielded Labs' first project and builds on an idea that developer Nate Wilcox proposed years ago, originally called the Zcash Posterity Fund, McGee said. A separate proposal under consideration would require Zcash Community Grants to contribute 50,000 ZEC from its treasury to the NSM.

— Jason McGee 🛡 (@aquietinvestor) November 5, 2026

NU7 also limits how much private transaction activity can fit into each block, reducing the data wallets must scan to find their payments, according to the upgrade specification.

Shielded Labs' original design would have replaced Zcash's halvings with a smooth issuance curve. Community panels supported that change, but coinholders did not, so an alternative design that kept the halvings was developed instead.

"We still think the original design was simpler and better, but we respect the process that led us here," McGee wrote. "It should be difficult to change the protocol, and the process should resist capture by any single organization, group, or influential individual."

The upgrade arrives as Zcash's community debates the future of Zcash Community Grants (ZCG) and its share of the Dev Fund, the slice of block rewards that pays for development.

The five organizations that coordinate Zcash's coinholder voting, Project Tachyon, Shielded Labs, Valar Group, the Zcash Foundation and ZODL, have set out an expedited timeline for proposals and polling, saying "tensions were high" and that a drawn-out debate risked "deepening divisions within the Zcash community."

Proposals are due by November 8, with polls for coinholders and community panels running from November 16 to November 30. If rough consensus emerges, the groups will support a further network upgrade, NU 7.1, to implement it.

#5131. US Treasury walks back crypto surveillance ‘travel rule’ proposal

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

FinCEN and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on funds worth $250 or more.

The Treasury Department has withdrawn a controversial proposal that would have expanded reporting requirements for cryptocurrency transactions.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on transactions of $250 or more, decreasing that threshold from $3,000.

In a filing, FinCEN said it was withdrawing the proposed rule because more than five years had passed, the convertible virtual currency industry had changed and additional public input would be helpful.

"The Agencies believe that any future, potential amendments to the Recordkeeping Rule and Travel Rule related to CVC should be considered in light of these recent developments to ensure that such amendments are fit for purpose," FinCEN said.

When the rule was proposed, FinCEN had argued that bad actors were increasingly using smaller transactions to move illicit funds. However, crypto groups, like Coin Center, had warned that lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 would mean that more ordinary transactions would be subject to "warrantless financial surveillance."

In a blog on Friday, Coin Center said it was happy to see the rule proposal rescinded, along with another rule proposal that was withdrawn last month that would have imposed reporting and recordkeeping requirements on transactions involving self-hosted wallets.

"This follows Treasury’s withdrawal last month of the proposed 'unhosted wallet' and cryptocurrency mixing rules, both of which Coin Center had also opposed," the organization said. 'Taken together, these withdrawals close the books on several long-pending proposals that would have expanded the Bank Secrecy Act’s already extensive financial surveillance regime."

Crypto Council for Innovation CEO Ji Hun Kim said in a statement that withdrawing the travel rule was the "right call."

"It had not moved forward over the past several years for good reason," he said. "Lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 for cross-border transfers would have placed undue burden on institutions."

HELLO, SHOULDER SEASON. Easily one of the best and (sometimes) overlooked times for camping, fall has all kinds of intrigue. From fewer people and colorful foliage to a crispness to the air and the possibility of high-elevation snow, there’s so much to be drawn to.

Sure, the temperature will fall, but that’s no reason to stow away the sleeping bag and tent for the year. Instead, add a few pieces of gear here and there to set yourself up for the best fall camping outing imaginable. That could be anything from a proper car camping mattress to another layer of apparel. So, keep that camp stove within reach as we slip into November and December. There’s a decent chance your favorite campsite is still open and better still, not subject to a waiting list.

Ourselves, we’ve still been sleeping in the outdoors and trekking on trails—it’s a year-round pursuit of testing gear for us. #5128. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

A former FBI agent has pleaded guilty to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency using wallet credentials the bureau had collected during its own investigations, the Justice Department said on Thursday.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled to be sentenced on March 4, 2027.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, pled guilty today to wire fraud and money laundering related to using his access to steal cryptocurrency.@FBIWFO https://t.co/jDVtSiAM3H

— U.S. Attorney EDVA (@EDVAnews) November 5, 2026

Yaroch worked at the FBI's Boston Division from 2017 to early 2025 on a squad investigating an unnamed "adversarial nation state," before moving to the bureau's Counterintelligence and Espionage Division, where he had access to FBI systems holding crypto credentials gathered in investigations, according to prosecutors.

Beginning no later than December 2024, he used that access to steal crypto from wallets controlled by subjects of other investigations, by nation-state actors, and from at least one wallet the FBI had created for an operation. He made about 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials from 35 separate FBI investigations, then swapped the stolen funds into other cryptocurrencies and moved them through further transactions to hide their origin. He used some of the money to pay off credit cards, car payments and other loans.

Yaroch also planned a new life abroad. He asked ChatGPT how to maximize profits from investments and where to live in the European Union, and after the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to obtain a tax identification number for him, prosecutors said. He arranged to travel to Portugal in September.

His actions compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, as well as sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection, according to the plea agreement's statement of facts.

Yaroch turned himself in. On July 29, he broke down while telling a former colleague at the Justice Department's National Security Division that he had made "very poor decisions" involving crypto wallets, according to an FBI affidavit filed in the case. He then self-reported to FBI headquarters, saying he had become frustrated that the bureau "could not or would not act" against an adversary's crypto accounts and had decided to "take matters into his own hands."

At the time, Yaroch said he had made about 10 to 12 transfers worth under $1 million, far less than the figures in his plea. Agents searching his home two days later seized a Trezor hardware wallet, three passports and a slip of paper bearing seed phrases, according to the affidavit. His Kraken account held about $188,600, mostly in U.S. dollars and the USDC stablecoin, with smaller amounts in Squid (QUID) and Bitcoin. A further $933,800 sat in Suilend, a lending protocol on the Sui blockchain, which he had accessed through the Slush wallet app. Yaroch told agents he chose Suilend "simply because he liked that the logo was a water droplet."

Agents transferred about $925,000 from those accounts to government-controlled wallets, the affidavit said. The roughly $165,600 held as U.S. dollars on Kraken could not be moved the same way.

Decrypt reported on the charges against Yaroch in August.

#5126. Treasury cuts off MEXC Global over alleged $16 billion in transactions linked to Iran, North Korea and Russia

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued an order against MEXC Global and said it was a money laundering concern.

The U.S. Treasury Department says it has severed crypto exchange MEXC Global from the United States after it allegedly supported $16 billion in transactions connected to Iran, North Korea, and Russia.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued an order against MEXC Global and said it was a money laundering concern.

"Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system," said Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent in a statement. "Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets."

Over the past year, Treasury has implemented a crackdown of sorts on crypto platforms allegedly helping Iran evade sanctions amid its ongoing war against the country. In June, Iran's largest crypto exchange, Nobitex, was sanctioned after Treasury said it processed more than half of Iran's crypto inflows in 2025. In September, the U.S. also sanctioned Iranian crypto exchange BitBank over alleged bitcoin transfers to the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

On Friday, Treasury said that though MEXC Global said it maintained anti-money laundering and says it will block the use of people using it in countries including Iran and North Korea, that it had "severe deficiencies" in those AML controls. The department said Friday's sanction will protect the U.S. financial system.

"Today's action will protect the U.S. financial system from the significant illicit finance threats posed by MEXC Global," the department said.

#5123. The 6 Best Beard Trimmers for Men in 2026, Tested by Grooming Editors

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Expert-approved trimmers from Panasonic, Bevel, and more.

THE FIRST STEP to a righteous beard? Grow one. Like, don’t touch your facial hair at all for at least six weeks. The second step—once you have enough length—trim it up. The growing part is easy, but once you get to styling, things get a bit more complicated. Whether you want a long, flowing beard or something shorter and cleaner, using the right beard products to keep your facial fur in shape also ensures your whiskers stay strong and healthy. And no tool is more important than a quality beard trimmer.

I’m one of those weird dudes who had a full beard by the time I left middle school, so I’ve been guinea pigging beard trimmers since before I was doing trigonometry—which, to be fair, extends to this day (is that the one with shapes?). In the years since, I have tried out just about every beard style you can think of: long grisly beard, tight stubble, even a goatee for a short period of time after seeing the first Iron Man movie (I have had all photographic evidence either scrubbed from the internet or burnt in a fire, so don’t even try).

Through this all, a beard trimmer was the most consequential tool in my grooming arsenal, with the best options changing with each style. Nothing else can give you a uniform cut or easily shape or fade a beard the way a trimmer can, no matter the beard’s style. But beyond length possibilities, you’ll want to consider your hair type and where you are in your own personal facial hair journey. For example, if your beard is coarse or very thick, you’ll need a more powerful trimmer like the Bevel trimmer or Braun Series 9.

If you’re new to growing a beard, you might want to look on the simpler side to make sure you get the hang of beard trimming before you invest, like with the Philips Norelco OneBlade. And if you’re a beard veteran, you’ll want something more professional-grade (like the Panasonic trimmer) and fly without the protection of guards. Then there are the specifics of each device (including how easy it is to maintain), which will really determine the best beard trimmer for you to invest in.

I’ve never been shy about how much I love Braun’s grooming devices, especially those with Series 9 in the title. So, of course, I love the All-in-One Series 9 Beard Trimmer and its insanely versatile design. Capable of efficiently tackling beards of any length, not to mention body grooming and even head hair, the Series 9 is the most wide-ranging option on this list.

Coming with ten (!) adjustments, it can tackle any beard style you’re going for. Plus, it has adjustments for other hair jobs, including ones as delicate as trimming nose hairs or as bold as a full crew cut.

What Else to Consider: This trimmer is truly an all-in-one device that can be used on hair from head to toe. I don’t typically recommend using the same device on your face as you do on your body for sanitary reasons, but if you’re in a jam or less worried about cross-contamination (you should be, though), this device is capable of handling any beard or body grooming.

If you are dedicated to sticking to one trimmer for all of your grooming needs, I recommend at least committing to attachments for each area to limit the exchange of bacteria, and this kit has more than enough to do so. That being said, while it's a solid option for touch-ups, the Series 9 lacks the power of true hair clippers to become a regular set for at-home haircuts.

Why We Chose It: The Braun Series 9 is essentially a luxury, souped-up version of the King C. Gillette Pro. It features a similar design with an easy-to-use adjustment dial that makes switching between desired lengths a breeze. When it comes to beard trimming, whether short or long, it performs with the best of them, plowing through hair quickly, not to mention gently.

I had no issues with pulling or tugging, even when tackling multiple months of beard growth using this trimmer. I also appreciated the stellar hard-shell case the Series 9 comes with, as well as the slick charging dock that takes up less space than any other charging station I’ve tried—for beard trimmers or otherwise.

Long-Term Results: Braun claims that these blades are meant to last a lifetime without ever being replaced. While I can’t confirm that this is true, I’ve been using the same ones for over a year, and they still feel like new. However, Braun also claims that this tool has a three-hour runtime per charge; I found it lasts more accurately in the two-hour range.

While still impressive, it's not quite the full 180 minutes they advertise. Braun also doesn’t disclose the motor speed of the Series 9 trimmer, which is strange given how strongly I can confirm it is. Regardless, the power behind this trimmer hasn’t lessened at all over a year of regular use.

Read More: Braun All-in-One Series 9 Style Kit Review

The reason I love this Men’s Health Grooming Award-winning trimmer so much is that it’s just so freaking easy to use. The adjustable dial allows you to toggle between a whopping 19 different length settings without switching attachments, allowing for a truly customized beard trimming experience. No matter what beard length or style you're going for, this is your one-stop tool. The powerful motor powers 9,800 cuts per minute, and the stainless steel blades slice through even the thickest whiskers like butter.

What Else to Consider: The design of this trimmer is pretty genius. It takes away the need to switch out attachments frequently (and the need to somehow store multiple attachments). The rotary dial is convenient and ideal for beginners or people who have pretty straightforward beard shapes that don’t need a lot of finessing.

Experienced users who are used to using more traditional attachments may find there is a learning curve to using this razor and may take a bit of time to understand how the numbers on the dials correspond to different lengths. I, personally, like the ease of use and the innovation, but keep in mind that it may take some getting used to.

Why We Chose It: This trimmer received high marks in my testing process for almost every factor. The motor is one of the most powerful I've tried, and it works well on all types of beards, from thick to thin and especially short to medium-length styles. I found that the battery life is long, and I needed to recharge it less frequently than other models.

The single attachment seemed odd at first, but paired with the rotary dial, it is innovative and easy to use—especially if you frequently switch between lengths. The one downside is that, while the trimmer itself is well-designed and easy to hold, it is slightly too large to comfortably fit in my Dopp kit, making it a little hard to travel with if you’re concerned about saving space.

Long-Term Results: As I've continued to use this trimmer, it just keeps getting easier. Once you get over any learning curve that could come from adjusting to the rotary dial instead of traditional length attachments, the single-length attachment is convenient and very fast to use. Even though I've been using this for months consistently, the battery life has not waned, and the motor and blades still feel as strong as they did when I first used it. Overall, I'm happy with the investment.

This trimmer is the latest version of my favorite budget-friendly beard trimmer. It also features an easy-to-use rotary dial that allows you to switch quickly between lengths and has two different length attachments—one for short styles and one for medium to longer styles. Between the attachments and the wheel, you get an incredible 40 possible lengths to choose from at 0.5 mm increments, which means you can potentially get lengths that not many other trimmers can deliver, allowing you a truly customizable experience.

The trimmer itself, while the brand doesn’t disclose the strength of the motor or the type of metal the blades are made from, feels substantial, and it’s powerful enough to deliver a good trim on pretty much any beard, especially at this price point.

What Else to Consider: Apart from the 40 different lengths you can get with this trimmer, there aren’t a lot of other bells and whistles to speak of. It is rechargeable (with the included power cord) and delivers a long battery life (45 days) with a single charge, but a full charge takes several hours, so if you’re going to travel with it, make sure it’s charged completely before you pack it. The plastic attachments can feel a little flimsy, but they still get the job done once they are attached to the trimmer. It also includes a storage bag, which is handy for both storage and travel.

Why We Chose It: Out of all the trimmers I tried at this price point, this one had the best performance, full stop. Also, you can’t argue with the sheer amount of possible length settings, which blows all the other similarly priced options out of the water. The motor and blades aren’t quite as powerful as higher-priced models, but it still performs well, making it a good option for beard newbies and experienced beard trimmers alike.

Long-Term Results: After months of use, I haven’t noticed any wane in the actual performance of the trimmer when it comes to, you know, trimming. The battery does need charging more often as time goes on, but it still holds up well even if you use this every day. I can’t say I use all 40 length options, well, ever, but they’re good to have if I ever want to experiment a bit. Mostly, I just keep it at the same length all the time, and that’s totally fine by me.

This handy trimmer is one of the most versatile options out there. It can do it all—shave, trim, detail, and more. Use the length guards to clean up short beards or perma-stubble looks. The innovative non-irritating shaver can also detail neck and cheek lines, clean up sideburns, and pretty much anything else you could think of doing. Thanks to its innovative signature 360 blade head that resembles more a Swiffer Sweeper than a standard electric razor, it's able to contort to every angle on your face—moving side to side or up and down—without risking any nicks or cuts.

The compact, lightweight design is super travel-friendly—just toss it into your Dopp kit or toiletry bag and forget about it. With a 45-minute rechargeable battery, you might even be able to leave the cord at home. It's completely waterproof, so it's safe for shower use, and each blade lasts up to four months.

The best part? It's less than $40. Due to its rectangular design and ultra-soft blades, this trimmer struggles with length and can’t really handle bigger jobs, so long beard aficionados may want to look elsewhere. But, for guys who like to keep their beard, mustache, or stubble game tight, this is a damn-near-perfect solution.

What Else to Consider: Keep in mind that the OneBlade is a do-it-all device. Yes, it can trim your (shorter) beard, but it can also shave, and you can even use it all over your body. If you do choose to take it below the neck, this is where the extra blade can come in handy. You don’t want to use the same blades on your body as you do on your face, especially if you use it on your body first. Doing so could run the risk of bacterial cross-contamination, and no one wants something like jock itch on their face (it happens!).

Why We Chose It: In terms of sheer versatility, I really stand behind the OneBlade. Okay, so it’s not the best for guys with longer beards, but for the vast majority of guys out there, especially if you rock the perma-stubble or a short beard look, it’s hard to beat (not to mention it also really shaves).

The blades are also multi-directional, meaning they cut on both ends instead of just one like a traditional trimmer, making it easy to use even if you’ve never used an electric shaver or trimmer before. The sleek, simple design is both easy to hold and easy to travel with, which is always a plus for us.

Long-Term Results: I've been using the OneBlade for years (really), and it remains one of my go-to grooming gadgets. I will say that you really do need to replace the blades every so often (the brand recommends every 4 months, though I’ve found they can last a bit longer in the six-month range), but luckily, they’re much easier to replace than your typical trimmer or shaver. Other than replacing the blades, my OneBlade works just the same as it did right out of the box—even years later.

Read More: Philips Norelco OneBlade Review

What’s great about this trimmer is that it’s deceptively simple. It’s a sleek, well-designed package that feels tightly edited and thoughtful in how you actually want to use a trimmer. But inside the casing is a powerhouse that plows through hair, even thick beards, easily, thanks to the 7,200 RPM motor and a specially angled blade that helps to lift up hairs and make it easier to cut them.

There’s only one attachment, but combined with the integrated rotary dial, you get 20 possible cutting lengths, allowing you freedom to customize your look, even if your beard is on the longer side.

What Else to Consider: This trimmer does have a good amount of bells and whistles, but nothing that feels extraneous. It uses a standard USB-C cord to charge up, so you don’t have to worry about losing a special cord. That’s a subtle LED indicator to let you know when your charge is getting low, and the charge speed is fast.

There’s a full 60 minutes of run time with a single charge, so depending on how long it takes you to trim your beard, it can last you a while. I also appreciate that it comes with a travel case included, and since this is my favorite trimmer to travel with, it gets a lot of use.

Why We Chose It: Did I mention this is my favorite trimmer to travel with? That’s because it’s sleek and simple, so you don’t need to pack a bunch of extra attachments in the included travel case. Plus, with the USB-C cord to charge, I can use the one I'm already packing if I need it (but I usually don’t because the battery life is good). It feels much more expensive than it actually is, which is always a plus in our book.

Long-Term Results: I've been using this trimmer for over a year now, and I'm still impressed by it. It really has become my go-to travel trimmer because of its simplicity paired with power. I haven’t noticed any dip in battery power or cutting prowess so far.

Read More: Manscaped Lawn Mower 5.0 Review

If you’re an experienced beard groomer or have a longer beard that you don’t need attachments to trim, this trimmer is for you. It’s really a professional-grade clipper designed for at-home use, so don’t expect length guards. However, in the right hands, this trimmer is a fine, precise tool that does it all. It can cut through thick whiskers to shape up a beard, shave in sharp lines and edges, and fade sideburns with ease.

It's pricey at $200, but again, it's basically a commercial barber-quality trimmer for the everyman. I love the sleek, ultra-modern design that's rare to find in most beard trimmers. It makes for a cool statement piece on any master vanity.

What Else to Consider: If you’re a beard newbie or have a short beard, keep on moving. This isn’t technically a beard trimmer—it’s more the kind of trimmer your barber uses to shape up your hairline and those sorts of things. But the reason it’s on this list is that if you have a long beard, many beard trimmer attachments won’t work for you, even the longest ones. Especially because long beards often need to be shaped freehand with a tool like this.

Why We Chose It: Long beards need tools, too! Plus, it’s great at creating crisp, clean lines for your beard, which many trimmers neglect in favor of cutting the body of the beard itself. This powerful trimmer can be used as a detailer and is great at line work. Even without attachments, it’s a good tool to have on hand if you like very crisp lines to your beard or if you have a long beard that a typical beard trimmer can’t support.

Long-Term Results: When I let my beard really grow, I'm constantly reaching back for this trimmer to manage it. The motor stays powerful, and the blade stays sharp even after months of use. I also regularly use it as a detailer. It’s a barber-level tool that creates sharp, crisp lines on our necks and cheeks, and I've found that even if I use a different trimmer to cut and shape my beard, I reach for this to clean up the edges and make sure they’re extra crisp.

Of course, ultimately what beard trimmer is best for you comes down to your personal needs and preferences (which is why this list has six trimmers, not just one). But there are a few things you can look for that are essential for any beard trimmer, no matter what sort of beard you're caring for. Here’s the lowdown on how to shop for the best beard trimmers in 2026.

You want a strong motor with enough juice to shave or cut to the right length cleanly, smoothly, and with a minimal number of passes (more passes make for more skin irritation). In most cases, you get what you pay for here. The most budget-friendly beard trimmers in our roundup (those under $50) all do a decent job, but they often can’t match the raw power of a more premium, $100-plus model.

This is why you’ll notice most budget options tend not to disclose their motor speed, while all the higher-end devices on this list start at either 6,000 rpm (rotations per minute) or 10,000 cuts per minute, depending on which metric they use to detail motor power.

Much like the motor, the blades of a trimmer will dictate how quickly and effectively it cuts through hair. Typically, you want to look for blades made of durable materials like stainless steel or metal that stay sharp for a long time (many trimmers will even have self-sharpening blades, which is a plus for longevity).

You will eventually need to replace the blades, so choosing one with replaceable blades will always be helpful, but blades made from strong materials, like metals, will last longer than those made of plastic.

Cordless trimmers are great for a lot of reasons, which makes them the preferred choice for most bearded gents. But they can’t match the sheer power of traditional, corded models. If you’re opting for the latter, consider models with a long, flexible power cord like Bevel’s. This makes them both easier to store and to maneuver around your face with better precision.

If you’re opting for a cordless model, it’s important to keep its battery life in mind. Knowing how often you’ll need to charge it can mean never needing to stop in the middle of a trim to recharge (believe us, it happens way more often than you’d think). If you can remember to charge your trimmer often, then battery life may not be super important to you, but if you can’t, travel a lot, or just don’t want the hassle, opting for one with a long battery life is important.

Of course, length attachments are important in a beard trimmer as they allow you the freedom to customize the length of your beard with ease (and ensure an even cut). Some beard trimmers include only the most essential tools and attachments, which is fine if you’re a beginner or just need the basics.

But other, more comprehensive kits are made more versatile with the addition of things like guide combs, extra trimmer heads, nose trimmer attachments, and more. Not having these isn’t necessarily a deal-breaker; again, it’s all about what’s right for you.

I barely remember the days before I had a beard, so you could say I’ve been testing trimmers for roughly two decades at this point. However, over the past two years, I’ve consulted with my fellow Men's Health editors and writers on the best-performing beard trimmers and which they prefer. As a grooming editor, I’m constantly testing the latest and greatest beard-trimming products, so I can also confirm that this list is up to date for the best options available in 2026.

Based on this experience, I was able to narrow down our search before I put a number of beard trimmers to the test to evaluate—or reevaluate—their effectiveness, durability, price point, and ease of use. The final guide below represents my honest picks of the six best beard trimmers worth buying in 2026. For some of the beard trimmers, I've also included before-and-after shots of yours truly so you can get a feel for the type of job each can handle.

Whether you’re buying your first beard trimmer or looking to upgrade, these are the half dozen worth investing in.

#5122. Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

📉 Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

On Wednesday, the market decided a Democratic Congress was good news. Stocks had their best day in three months, Bitcoin climbed back over $80,800, and Trump posted on Truth Social that the market “just hit another Record High” with 401(k)s “through the roof.”

On Thursday, the market changed its mind.

The S&P 500 fell 4.36%, its worst session since the Liberation Day tariffs in April 2025. The Nasdaq dropped 5.52% and closed in correction, more than 10% below its record from last month. The Dow shed 1,833 points.

The damage concentrated in one place. The iShares Semiconductor ETF fell more than 12%, its worst day since March 2020. Micron lost 17.5%, AMD 17.3%, Marvell 16.4%, Nvidia 9.3%. The Magnificent Seven shed roughly $1 trillion in market value. The VIX touched 28.09 intraday, its highest since March. By the close, the U.S. stock market had lost $3T in value - more than the entire market cap of crypto.

Notably, the thing that spooked investors was the same election result that lifted everything a day earlier. Several states where Democrats won have active local opposition to data center construction, and the market spent Thursday pricing in slower permitting and tighter oversight of the AI buildout. Ipek Ozkardeskaya at Swissquote put it simply: “We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout.” Rate expectations moved too, with December hike odds falling to 70.1% from 82.7%.

Crypto did not sit this one out. Bitcoin fell 5.4% to $75,871, below where it traded before the election. Ethereum dropped 7.6% to $2,239, a steeper fall than the Nasdaq. XRP lost 9.8%, and Hyperliquid fell 4.9%. The CMC20 index fell 6.29%. Almost $1.1B in crypto longs were liquidated on the day, before a slight relief rally overnight.

Friday’s October jobs report is next. After a week where the market read the same political outcome two opposite ways inside two sessions, it is fair to wonder how much anyone actually knows right now. We will find out soon…

Start every day with the top news stories right now, plus original features, a podcast, videos and more.

#5120. Binance, Deribit revoke SignalPlus API keys after fake Teams invite attack

SignalPlus said attackers compromised an engineer’s computer in a breach that resulted in losses of less than $82,000, which the crypto trading platform pledged to reimburse in full.

Exchanges Binance and Deribit said Thursday they had revoked API keys connected to crypto derivatives trading platform SignalPlus as a precaution following a security breach. Both exchanges said their own systems were unaffected, while Deribit warned that trading, monitoring and automation through SignalPlus had stopped working for its customers.

Binance co-founder Yi He also urged third-party platforms integrated with the exchange to review their security controls and monitor for suspicious activity, warning that the threat extended beyond any single company.

SignalPlus provides trading infrastructure for crypto derivatives, particularly options, allowing professional and institutional traders to manage positions across multiple exchanges through a single platform.

According to the company on X, the attack originated from a compromised Telegram account belonging to a contact at a third-party technology vendor. The attacker impersonated the contact and sent a fake Microsoft Teams meeting invitation to a SignalPlus engineer, compromising the employee’s computer.

The attacker remained dormant on the compromised computer before using the engineer’s access privileges to target SignalPlus’s internal network early Wednesday, the company said.

SignalPlus said it detected and contained the intrusion, with total losses of less than $82,000, and had begun fully reimbursing affected users. The company temporarily suspended its services and expects to restore operations over the next few days after completing security reviews and applying patches.

SignalPlus also acknowledged that it had not required IP whitelisting for exchange API access. “We take responsibility for this oversight,” the company said, adding that it would tighten API access requirements and strengthen internal security controls. IP whitelisting is a security measure that restricts API access to a predefined list of approved internet addresses.

Related: BitMart founder says security team ‘severely understaffed’ after new hack

The SignalPlus incident comes days after Binance disclosed details of a $6.3 billion hack on Oct. 29, which also involved compromised employee computers.

According to Binance’s postmortem, attackers posing as recruiters tricked an engineer into downloading a malicious file disguised as a coding assignment. The attackers subsequently used the compromised computer to gain access to additional systems and eventually breach the exchange’s wallet infrastructure.

Binance co-founder Yi He said Tuesday that the exchange had detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats in 2026, including fraudulent job interviews, malicious software packages and attempts to steal employee credentials.

#5119. Muhammad Ali at Home: 10 Photos of the Heavyweight Champion’s Life Outside the Ring

It was a stroke of luck—bad luck—that got Muhammad Ali into the ring for the first time. The Louisville, Kentucky, native was 12 years old when his brand-new bicycle was stolen. The police officer to whom Ali reported the theft happened to own a boxing gym—and became his first coach.

Ten years later, in 1964, Ali, then still known by his birth name, Cassius Clay, became the world heavyweight champion when he defeated reigning titleholder Sonny Liston. The athlete, who earned the nickname “The Greatest,” would go on to win that title two more times, as well as an Olympic gold medal and numerous honors for his civil rights activism and humanitarian work.

There was, of course, another side to the legend: his domestic life. As we gear up for the release of the Prime Video series The Greatest, which explores the prizefighter’s rise to prominence, we’re looking back at photos of Ali in his off-duty hours.

Days after becoming the heavyweight champion, Ali announced his conversion to the Nation of Islam, changed his name, and then relocated to Chicago, where the controversial religion’s headquarters are based. (He later converted to Sunni Islam). In 1967, Ali cited his religious beliefs when he refused to serve in the US Army during the Vietnam War. His boxing license revoked and his championship title stripped, Ali entered a period of exile. He is pictured in his Chicago home the following year, reading a book by Nation of Islam leader Elijah Muhammad. The athlete shared the dwelling with his second wife, Khalilah Camacho-Ali, whom he married at the residence on August 17, 1967. The single-story brick house was in the Avalon Park neighborhood and was a fully furnished gift from the Nation of Islam.

The boxer and his toddler Maryum feeding 10-week-old twins Rasheda and Jamillah in their Philadelphia home.

The couple welcomed a daughter, Maryum, in 1968, and two years later, twins named Rasheda and Jamillah. The newborns are pictured between Ali and Maryum at home in Pennsylvania in this November 1970 photograph. The fighter and his family stayed at the home, in West Philadelphia’s Overbrook Park neighborhood, for about a year. It was later owned by Kobe Bryant’s grandparents.

Ali with fans at his Pennsylvania training camp in August 1974.

Though he soon relocated to New Jersey, Ali would maintain ties to Pennsylvania for decades with his training camp, Fighter’s Heaven. He purchased the Deer Lake property in 1972, the year after the Supreme Court overturned his draft-evasion conviction, allowing him to regain his boxing license. On the land, he custom-built a log gym, a dining hall, several cabins, a mosque, and a courtyard with a large stone fireplace. His training sessions were open to the public; in this August 1974 photo, a group of fans are pictured behind him at the camp. He kept the retreat long after his retirement, selling it in 1997 to martial arts instructor George Dillman. The property is now a tourist destination, complete with a small museum.

Ali at home in Los Angeles before his fight with Larry Holmes.

Ali briefly retired from boxing at age 37 in 1979, but returned the following year to fight Larry Holmes. The boxer poses at his home office in Hancock Park, Los Angeles, in this August 1980 photo, taken about a month before his fight with Holmes.

Ali shoots pool in his Hancock Park house in this 1980 photo.

Ali shows off the game room in his Hancock Park, Los Angeles, home in this 1980 snapshot. Around this period, he started to show early symptoms of Parkinson’s disease, which impacted his athletic ability. He lost the fight to Holmes and went back into retirement.

The boxing champion and his wife Veronica at home in 1982.

In 1981, Ali fought one last time before hanging up his gloves. “I know it’s the end,” he said after losing the fight. “I’m not crazy. After Holmes, I had excuses. I was too light. Didn’t breathe right. No excuses this time. I’m happy. I’m still pretty. I could have a black eye. Broken teeth. Split lips. I think I came out all right for an old man.” Ali stands next to his third wife, Veronica Porché, in front of his Hancock Park dwelling in this photo, taken a few months after his final match.

The Greatest takes a call at home in 1982.

In this photo from the same March 1982 photo shoot, Ali strikes a pose in his opulent Los Angeles living room. The retired boxer had purchased the 14,500-square-foot home in 1979, two years after marrying Porché. The 1916 build, by architect John C. Austin, was an Italian Renaissance mansion complete with seven bedrooms.

The estate featured stained glass window panels designed by Louis Comfort Tiffany (the son of Tiffany & Co. founder Charles Lewis Tiffany) and a stately dining room with intricately carved dark wood and a crystal chandelier, as seen in this October 1985 photo of Ali eating cereal. That year, he and Porché filed for divorce.

Ali outfitted the home with a (packed) trophy room, which he shows off in this 1985 picture. He left the Hancock Park dwelling the following year, when his and Porché’s divorce was finalized. Shortly after, Ali married his fourth wife, Lonnie Williams. As his Parkinson’s disease progressed (he was officially diagnosed in 1984), Williams became Ali’s primary caregiver.

The former fighter has his picture taken by photographer Neil Leifer at home in Arizona’s Paradise Valley.

Ali poses for a photo shoot commemorating his 70th birthday in 2012 at the Arizona home he and Williams shared. That year, he was honored with the Liberty Medal for his humanitarian efforts, which he devoted more time to following his retirement. He was also awarded the Presidential Medal of Freedom in 2005. Ali died in Arizona in 2016 at 74.

#5117. Crypto ally French Hill wins reelection but set to lose House chair

Republican French Hill secured another term in Congress but will lose his leadership of the House Financial Services Committee when Democrats take control in January.

House Financial Services Committee Chair French Hill, a key Republican ally of the cryptocurrency industry, has won reelection in Arkansas but is expected to lose his committee chairmanship after Democrats won control of the House of Representatives in Tuesday’s midterm elections.

The Associated Press called the race Thursday, confirming Hill’s victory over Democratic challenger Chris Jones in Arkansas’ 2nd Congressional District. Hill, who sponsored the CLARITY Act and voted for the GENIUS Act, holds an A rating from the crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto, which classifies him as strongly supportive of cryptocurrency.

Fairshake, a cryptocurrency industry-backed super political action committee (PAC), pledged $1 million to support Hill’s reelection campaign in October as part of its efforts to retain crypto-friendly lawmakers in Congress.

California Democrat Maxine Waters, the current ranking member of the House Financial Services Committee, is the leading contender to replace Hill as chair, according to Reuters reporting.

Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress in Tuesday’s midterm elections, raising uncertainty over the future of US crypto legislation and the prospect of increased industry scrutiny. Crypto-backed super PACs spent more than $176 million during the election cycle.

#5116. How to Build Your Legs With the Glute-Ham Raise

This underrated exercise could be the MVP of your lower body training.

IF YOU’RE A dedicated lifter, your lower body doesn’t lack attention in your workouts. You might even dedicate an entire session to it each week (hello, leg day). But just because your training plan is packed with deadlifts, squats, lunges, calf raises, hip thrusts, and step-ups doesn’t mean you’re doing everything you can to maximize your leg development. You probably walk right past one of the most powerful lower body machines every time you’re at the gym: the glute ham developer (GHD).

It might look like a gussied-up horizontal Roman chair, but the GHD is a different beast entirely. Instead of targeting your glutes and lower back with hyperextensions, the GHD allows you to laser focus on your biceps femoris, semitendinosus, and semimembranosus (collectively known as your hamstrings) with an exercise called the glute ham raise (GHR). It’s so effective at what it does that only the deadlift can rival its hamstring-building power, making it a formidable addition to any leg day. Use these tips to weave it into your routine for maximum results.

AS MENTIONED ABOVE, you’ll need a GHD to perform a GHR. Where a Roman chair puts your body at a 45-degree angle and only allows movement in the hips, the GHD elevates you off the floor, locking your ankles between two sets of rollers while your knees (in the starting position) and then thighs (in the down position) rest on rounded pads. In so doing, it shifts the emphasis from your lower back to the backs of your legs, requiring you to use your hamstrings to lower and raise your body primarily through knee flexion as you perform the GHR.

How well does it work? A small research trial suggests the glute ham raise is so effective at targeting your hamstrings that it’s on par with the Romanian deadlift and superior to such leg-sculpting staples as the leg curl and good morning.

And the benefits don’t stop there. In addition to your hams, you’ll light up muscles all along your posterior chain, including your calves, glutes, and erector spinae. Even your abs will kick into gear to help your erector spinae support and stabilize your spine.

IF YOUR STRENGTH program is divided into training splits, add the GHR to your leg day. If your weekly routine skews more toward total body workouts, incorporate the GHR into one or two of them, making sure it’s among a diverse selection of leg exercises spread across three or four strength sessions. Either way, it’s a good idea to perform it toward the end of a workout after heavier compound lifts like the squat and deadlift.

You can load the GHD by holding a weight plate to your chest, but start with only your bodyweight, following the instructions below until you master the movement.

In a true GHR, the lowering phase of the movement stops when your body is horizontal to the floor. Going past that point by hinging downward at your hips until your torso is perpendicular to the floor adds a back extension element and causes you to lose hamstring tension, undercutting your hamstring gains—so make sure you stay disciplined with your range of motion.

*If you don’t have access to a GHD, you can enlist a buddy to hold your ankles on the floor or use a specialized bench to perform a Nordic curl, which also requires your hamstrings to work overtime as you lower and raise your thighs and torso through knee flexion.

Trevor Thieme is a Los Angeles-based writer and strength coach, and a former fitness editor at Men’s Health. When not helping others get in shape, he splits his time between surfing, skiing, hiking, mountain biking, and trying to keep up with his 10-year-old daughter.

#5115. Tesla to Wind Down Cybertruck Production as Elon Musk Pivots to Robotaxis and AI

Tesla to Wind Down Cybertruck Production as Elon Musk Pivots to Robotaxis and AI

Tesla will stop making the Cybertruck at its Texas Gigafactory around the middle of next year, CEO Elon Musk said on Friday, as the company shifts its focus to autonomous vehicles.

"Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production," Musk tweeted.

Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production

Responding to a user who called the Cybertruck the best vehicle Tesla has ever made, Musk replied: "True, but the Cybertruck fleet will be around for a long time." "Cybertruck was an incredible technology development platform and lives on through Cybercab," Wes Morrill, the truck's lead engineer, wrote in response to Musk's post, thanking the truck's owners and "everyone who contributed to making the future looking like the future."

The stainless-steel pickup, first delivered to customers in late 2023, has struggled to win buyers. Tesla delivered 8,295 vehicles in its "other models" category, which includes the Cybertruck, in the third quarter, down 48% from a year earlier, Electrek reported. That puts the truck's sales at roughly a tenth of the 250,000 units a year that Tesla built its production line to make, according to the outlet.

Musk has made the Cybercab, Tesla's robotaxi, central to his push to turn the carmaker into an AI and robotics company. In April 2025, he said Tesla would launch its first robotaxi service in Austin using Model Y vehicles running its Full Self-Driving software, and begin building Cybercabs in Texas the following year.

Tesla began offering rides in the Cybercab, a two-seater with no steering wheel or pedals that was first unveiled in October 2024, in limited areas of Austin on September 3, Reuters reported. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration said at the time that it was "evaluating the situation," as federal rules typically require cars to have human controls. Tesla began producing the Cybercab in April, and had 45 of them registered in Texas as of the launch, according to the report.

At the 2024 unveiling, Musk said he expects the Cybercab to cost around $30,000, Auto Express reported.

The shift comes as Musk weighs combining Tesla with SpaceX, which absorbed his AI company xAI earlier this year. Last month, Musk said both companies' boards had formed special committees of independent directors to evaluate a merger, which would reshape his AI empire by bringing rockets, satellites, AI models, robots and cars under one roof. A SpaceX filing has since confirmed that any combination would need sign-off from its "controlling shareholder," Musk.

A merger would also create a Bitcoin treasury behemoth, combining SpaceX's 18,712 BTC and Tesla's 11,509 BTC for a total of 30,221 BTC, worth roughly $2.3 billion at current prices.

Both companies have also blocked their shares from being offered on the tokenized stock venue being built by crypto exchange OKX and NYSE owner ICE.

#5113. Asian crypto exchanges batten down the hatches as hacks surge: Asia Express

Asian crypto exchanges are scrambling to tighten security on fears of a wave of potentially AI-assisted hacks, even as regional governments embrace tokenization and stablecoins.

India’s national cybersecurity agency warned that an AI-assisted threat actor has escalated its attacks on cryptocurrency exchanges and other financial businesses, stealing wallet keys and siphoning funds.

The Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In) on Wednesday designated the threat actor as “CRAFTY FALCON” and explicitly identified crypto exchanges among affected businesses.

“The threat is active and ongoing. CSIRT-Fin continues to receive reports of successful attacks, including from NBFCs and fintech entities that had already received the earlier restricted advisories and had undertaken remediation,” said CERT-In.

The warning adds to evidence of hackers using AI tools against financial institutions. In October, cybersecurity firm CrowdStrike reported that an unidentified attacker used ARTEX, an AI penetration-testing tool also named in CERT-In’s advisory, alongside Claude Code and other AI models to breach South Korean financial firms and steal data.

South Korea’s Financial Services Commission has urged domestic crypto exchanges to increase the proportion of assets held in cold wallets after the $6.3 billion attack on Binance.

The FSC said it held an emergency meeting with other regulators, cybersecurity agencies and crypto service providers to review security measures.

It reviewed exchanges’ IT access controls, wallet withdrawal approval and signing procedures, and administrator privileges. It also urged providers to increase the proportion of assets held in offline cold wallets.

But South Korean law already requires exchanges to hold at least 80% of users’ crypto assets in cold storage, with Upbit keeping 99.17% assets in cold wallets, followed by Bithumb at 94.76% and Coinone at 85%.

The Bank of Korea is surveying 31 financial institutions on how a major exchange shutdown, prolonged crypto downturn or losses at another firm could affect their operations.

One scenario asks how financial firms would be affected if South Korea’s largest crypto exchange by market share temporarily suspended operations following a major hack or similar incident.

Other questions address a steep fall in cryptocurrency prices followed by a sustained trading slump, and the potential fallout if another financial institution suffered heavy losses from a crypto-related incident.

Despite clear evidence of years of North Korea-linked hacking groups stealing billions from crypto exchanges, Pyongyang claims to have had no involvement in the $6.3 billion Binance hack and floated the idea it was actually the Americans.

“The FBI is trying to brand the DPRK as a ‘cyber criminal state’, claiming that it obtained information that a hacking organization ‘supported’ by the DPRK was involved in the incident,” said Kim Yo Jong said. “But the US assertion is an absurd rhetoric and nothing new.”

South Korea’s government has backed postponing tax on cryptocurrency gains by another two years to 2029. Judging on the troubled history of the tax, it would not be surprising if it is delayed again at that point.

Crypto.com will discontinue its consumer app services in Hong Kong on Feb. 1, 2027, while continuing to pursue regulatory approval for its institutional exchange.

A Crypto.com spokesperson told Cointelegraph that the company would discontinue its consumer app services in Hong Kong. The spokesperson said all affected customers had received advance notice and would receive instructions on closing their accounts and support to withdraw their funds.

Nearly three years after the local entity submitted an application for a license and the status remains “deemed-to-be-licensed” — which a transitional status rather than full regulatory approval.

The Hong Kong headquartered Animoca Brands has secured approval to manage investment funds for non-retail clients in Abu Dhabi.

The authorization, granted to subsidiary Animoca Asset Management (AD) Limited by Abu Dhabi Global Market’s Financial Services Regulatory Authority (FSRA), allows it to oversee funds that pool money from professional investors rather than the general public.

It expanding the asset management business of a company that began in mobile games before moving into blockchain gaming, rode the Metaverse wave and was a big player in venture investing.

Hong Kong has expanded its Project Ensemble pilot to allow round-the-clock settlement of tokenized transactions, as the city’s monetary regulator prepares to bring regulated stablecoins into trials involving tokenized government debt.

Hong Kong has recently officially introduced secondary market trading for SFC-authorised Tokenized Funds through licensed exchanges. Two China Asset Management money-market funds can now trade continuously via PantherTrade and the Futubull app.

Thailand’s securities regulator has filed a criminal complaint against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, for allegedly operating without a license. Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission will block access to the website and app from December 5.

According to the SEC, Bitget offered crypto trading services between 2022 and 2025, charged trading fees and promoted its services in Thai through its websites and social media channels. The regulator said these activities violated Thailand’s digital asset business licensing requirements.

The regulator advised users to take action regarding assets held on the platform before the deadline.

Australia’s central bank raised its interest rate by 25 basis points to 4.85% on Tuesday, warning that inflation would stay elevated through much of 2027 as energy costs and AI-related investment added to price pressures.

The Reserve Bank of Australia’s monetary policy board voted 6–3 for the increase, with three members preferring to hold rates at 4.60%.

The bank pushed back its forecast for inflation to return to the top of its 2% to 3% target range until late 2027, around three quarters later than previously expected.

The Australian government’s decision to scrap the 50% capital gains discount for all investments in the May budget came as a big shock for crypto owners. The new scheme effectively doubles the taxable component of a gain, minus a small discount for inflation.

Some Aussie crypto holders are taking drastic measures — like Finder founder and crypto king Fred Schebesta who is relocating to Singapore which offers 0% CGT, stating the tax changes were a “killer blow.”

But there are less dramatic methods to reduce CGT bills including setting up a self managed super fund (SMSF), a company to trade crypto at the company tax rate, or to hope the opposition will reverse the changes after the next election.

Japanese yen stablecoin JPYC marked its first anniversary with a circulating supply around 3 billion yen—one quarter of its internal target.

BlackRock, BlackRock Japan, SBI Holdings and Startale have signed an MOU to explore connecting tokenized money-market funds with SBI’s JPYSC yen stablecoin.

Ministry of Finance rules require cryptocurrency exchanges licensed in Vietnam to report security incidents and remediation plan within 24 hours

Five major banks—including HSBC, JPMorgan’s Kinexys, MUFG, Société Générale-Forge under BNP Paribas, and Standard Chartered—have launched the Global Layer One Organization (GL1) in Singapore. The non-profit aims to promote standards for tokenized financial markets.

#5111. This DC Designer’s Nearly 200-Year-Old Virginia Farmhouse Flourishes With Detail and History

In the foothills of Virginia’s Blue Ridge Mountains, just minutes from Shenandoah National Park, the town of Sperryville (population: 350) still feels like a well-kept secret. In recent years, it’s attracted the eye of Washington, DC–based designer Melissa Colgan of Melissa Colgan Interiors, who purchased a weekend home there in 2023.

Though born in the Midwest, the designer and her husband, Daniel Schned, moved to DC in 2016 and spent their early years and weekends exploring the surrounding countryside. “One of the areas we fell in love with is Rappahannock County,” she says of discovering its scenic pastures, rolling hills, and grazing cows. “It feels a world away from DC.”

The staircase is covered in Schumacher’s Caterpillar Leaf print and the stair runner is by Sinclair Till. The cloche is custom by Dan Nakamura.

In the family room, the BDDW sofa is upholstered in a Décors Barbares fabric. The side table is from Covesville Antiques and topped with a lamp by Christopher Spitzmiller. The artwork is by Molly van Amerongen.

The fruitwood dining table from 1stDibs is surrounded with chairs by Lorenzo Castillo and upholstered in a fabric by Schumacher. The 1930s chandelier has custom shades covered in a Decors Barbares fabric. The chairs are by Leah O’Connell (left) and Soane. The artwork is by Joshua Perkin.

In 2020, the couple eloped at The Inn at Little Washington and began looking for a vacation home in the area. Three years later, they found a listing for an 1830s farmhouse and barn, which happened to be the former home and office of the county doctor. “It’s a very special house in Sperryville because it’s where the doctor saw all his patients in the early 1900s,” Colgan says.

What began as an 1830s single-room log cabin evolved to include a barn and second story in the 1890s, followed by another addition in the late 1990s. “A lot of the original details were still there,” Colgan says of the 1,200-square-foot residence. “We just wanted to freshen things up a bit.”

In the family living room, the windows, along with the original chink log siding, date to the 1830s. “I wanted to lean into some of the depth that was already there,” Colgan says of her color palette. “That prompted me into the world of deep reds, ochres, and blues.”

In a cozy corner, a Casamidy chair covered in soft mohair flanks a Swedish Gustavian oak secretary from the 1800s.

Colgan solicited the help of DC architect Alexander Smith of Mortar & Thatch. “The thing we aligned with from the very beginning is we didn't want it to feel like a new renovation,” Smith says. “Maybe we slapped on some paint or got new countertops, but we didn’t want it to seem like we were implanting foreign ideas.”

The biggest task was subdividing the single bedroom upstairs to accommodate Colgan’s growing family. At the time, she was pregnant with her now two-year-old daughter. “We basically took down the walls of the one bedroom, made a middle bedroom for our daughter, then eked every inch we could in order to fit a king bed and built-in closets in our room,” she says. The result is a quaint and colorful two-bedroom blueprint, with dappled light dancing through the street-facing windows.

In the pocket-size half bath, an American Federal mahogany mirror broadens the space, which is wrapped in Iksel Mogul Berry Wallpaper by Schumacher and adorned with an oil painting by Jay Harper.

Incorporating open shelves in the kitchen helped maximize storage while enabling the designer to showcase more of the home’s attractive chink log siding. Farrow & Ball’s Terre de Egypte paint accentuates Colgan’s collections of splatterware mugs and vintage objects, like an early-20th-century painter’s palette.

Today, they spend most of their time in the living room downstairs, with its original chink log siding from the 1830s. “The room is so soulful. You just walk in and take a deep breath,” she says. “The thing that came to mind early on was a little Russian country house or dacha. When we first saw the listing, we were in Switzerland, so that was the original seed for the design.”

Drawing from the warmth and depth of the living room’s original pine floors and log siding, Colgan conjured a rich color palette of ochres, reds, and blues with botanical and floral-patterned fabrics that mirrored the pastoral landscape. “We’re surrounded by mountains and flora and fauna, so I wanted the house to reflect that as well.”

A brisbee curtain in Lake August’s flowering Nasturtium fabric adds light and whimsy to the kitchen’s neutral cabinetry.

The kitchen pendants are by Urban Electric. The walls are painted in Dimity and the cabinets are painted Joa’s White, both by Farrow & Ball. The rug is by Matt Camron.

The furnishings are an array of old, new, and local finds from their 1920s French fruitwood dining table to the early American rocking chair salvaged from Ruckersville Antiques. “We didn’t really have anything for this house because the style is so different from our city home, so we had to start from scratch,” Colgan says. Still, the designer is an avid collector and had some pieces sitting in storage like the BDDW sofa, purchased at auction, that’s now in her living room. Currently, it’s upholstered in Décors Barbares’ lively Roses Tchékhonine print, with winged butterflies flitting across a canvas of cobalt and crimson flowers.

In the primary bedroom, every inch mattered. “We did multiple layouts and wanted to make sure the details, finishes, and everything in there was an appropriate scale so things didn’t feel too big or too small for the space,” says Smith. The walls and his-and-her closets are painted in Farrow & Ball’s Pink Ground and Eating Room Red, with Roman shades done in a Lucrezia fabric by Fortuny. An acrylic and ink work on paper by artist Julie Wolfe, which Colgan gave to her husband for their first wedding anniversary, hangs over the bed.

In the sunlit bathroom, blue and white garden tiles from Marble Systems bloom alongside fittings by Waterworks, D Porthault patterned towels, and an antique French mirror, circa 1785.

Other spaces, like the mudroom and laundry located near the home’s side entrance, were made more welcoming and useful, with cupboards dressed in wholesome, hay-colored gingham skirts. Reconfiguring appliances and adding cabinets and open shelving helped optimize the kitchen’s storage and traffic flow. “We were able to inch out so much in such a small space,” Smith says.

Despite its demure size, the house today is a sensory retreat for the couple. “That first Thanksgiving was a real stress test,” Colgan says. The barn in the back, where the doctor kept his horse for making house calls across the county, was renovated by the previous owner to include a kitchenette, an open living room space, and sleeping quarters. “The barn had pigs in it up until the last owner, and he renovated it,” she says. “I’ve since layered in a lot of fabrics and antiques.”

In their daughter’s room, a late-19th-century faux-bamboo pine and bird’s eye maple bed is made up with D Porthault Pink Coeur’s linens and a one-of-a-kind PROJEKTITYYNY Wisteria baby quilt, stitched with Liberty fabrics. The bookshelf is from Crate & Barrel and the walls and windows are covered in Ottoline’s blue and brown Indian Tulip print.

The walls are covered in Caterpillar Leaf by Schumacher and the slipper chair is custom. The Roman shade is a fabric by Antoinette Poisson.

Off the home’s side entry, a laundry and mudroom papered in Beata Heuman’s Eyelash design is further brightened thanks to the café curtain and cabinet skirts in a jumbo gingham by Beata Heuman, finished with French grosgrain trim.

Ultimately, however, it was the historic farmhouse where Colgan concentrated her creative energy. “A lot of times people come into these old houses and instead of trying to think carefully about how to make modern life function there, they blow out walls and make everything open,” she says. Instead, she champions her home’s nuanced details, including planks of weathered wood that have patinated over time or the way sunlight shimmers off the 19th-century windows, with their panes of wavy glass. “They are very much not energy efficient,” she says, “but they are absolutely beautiful.”

The custom console is painted in Benjamin Moore’s Van Deusen Blue and the walls are covered in Beata Heuman’s Eyelash Spring. The 1960s mirror is from 1stDibs.

On the front porch, a five-piece set by Newell Jacobsen is upholstered in a sky-blue Lisa Fine Madras Performance fabric that complements the painted ceiling, and the neighbor’s vibrant home. “There are some fun houses on our street—the one across the street is yellow,” Colgan says. “I liked the idea of colors out there being a little brighter.”
