#5110. The Best Under-the-Radar Menswear Brands, According to Style Editors

Think of this as your shortcut to discovering your next favorite label.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

PERSONALLY, I LOVE discovering new men’s clothing brands because shopping at the same ole’ places time and time again can become redundant. I know what to expect, and sometimes, the latest collections are overhyped and don’t quite deliver. So when I’m scrolling through my social media feed and come across an ad about an emerging label or one that’s flown under the radar, I’m immediately clicking through to see what they’re about. I mean, who doesn’t love finding a brand before everyone else catches on? Before I know it, I’m going down a rabbit hole, browsing the entire site and adding things to my wishlist.

But as a style editor and menswear enthusiast, I’m not just looking for something new for the sake of it. I want to know: Does the brand offer something different from what I already own? Does it bring a fresh perspective to menswear? And, most importantly, is it worth spending my money on? The brands that have really caught my attention tend to check those boxes. So, if you’re looking to switch up your wardrobe and discover something beyond your usual rotation, these are the men’s clothing brands worth knowing about.

Octobre Editions is a French menswear label, founded in Paris in 2016. The brand makes quality everyday clothes that you can wear for years and won’t go out of style. Think fine sweaters, shirts, jackets and coats, sneakers, and sometimes a limited-edition collection.

If you want to look sharp without defaulting to a suit, Reiss is worth a look. The London-based brand specializes in making muted tones of sharp-cut blazers, merino-wool sweater polos, button-downs, and suede jackets that make it easy to dress for the office or dinner.

A great pair of jeans can carry an entire outfit; California-label PAIGE gives you plenty of high-end premium options to find your fit. From slimmer cuts to more relaxed silhouettes, the brand offers denim in a range of washes that play well with everything from a basic tee to a leather jacket. Also in the lineup are classic chinos, sweatshirts, and shackets.

Want to experiment with barrel-leg pants, roomier jackets, or other statement silhouettes without spending a fortune? GU—Uniqlo’s sister company—is worth browsing for affordable everyday pieces that are trend-forward with a unique Tokyo-inspired aesthetic.

Beyond Yoga is a premium, body-inclusive active lifestyle and athleisure brand that produces workout gear that feels just as good outside the gym. Among its offerings are ultra-soft, stretchy workout shirts and tanks, athletic shorts, sweatpants, and hoodies, designed to keep you comfortable whether you’re playing tennis or running errands.

NN.07, an exclusive menswear-only Copenhagen brand, makes clothes that are easy to mix and match, which comes in handy when you want more outfit options without buying an entirely new wardrobe. From knitwear and shirts to trousers and outerwear, its pieces can work across casual plans and occasions that call for a slightly dressier look.

Did you know that Aritzia makes men’s clothing, too? The Canadian fashion design house has introduced cozy sweats and outerwear designed to handle cold temperatures and unpredictable weather. Its puffer jackets and rain jackets are worth a look if you need functional layers for chilly commutes or weekend hikes when the forecast isn't cooperating.

California Arts takes familiar menswear staples and gives them a different point of view through unexpected cuts and proportions. Take its Henleys, which feature an extra-deep button placket or its outerwear, which reworks classic coats with oversized shapes, dramatic lengths, and distinctive details like belted closures. Despite the unconventional silhouettes, many of the pieces can still work with wardrobe staples like straight-leg jeans and leather shoes.

#5108. $3.1B in Stolen Binance Crypto Has Yet to Be Laundered: Elliptic

$3.1B in Stolen Binance Crypto Has Yet to Be Laundered: Elliptic

Around half of the $6.43 billion stolen from Binance last month has yet to be laundered, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said in a report updated on Friday.

About $3.11 billion remains in addresses linked to the theft, mostly in ETH and Bitcoin, and those funds are "very likely" still held by North Korea, Elliptic said. A further $2.85 billion, or 44% of the haul, has been frozen or seized, mostly stablecoins that issuers such as Tether and Circle froze within an hour of the theft. That compares with around 3% frozen after the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025, according to Elliptic.

Binance hack update: the XRP has started moving. About $50 million has been swapped, mostly through THORChain into BTC, with about $226 million still to move.

Bitcoin converted from other stolen assets is now about 1,650 BTC ($124 million), almost twice the figure a day earlier.…

The FBI has attributed the October 29 theft to North Korean hackers it calls TraderTraitor. North Korea has denied involvement.

The laundering so far has been "highly complex and clearly automated," Elliptic said. It has used more than 20,000 wallets on 16 blockchains, at least 36 services and at least 65,000 transactions, roughly one new wallet every 30 seconds.

The 18,270 BTC stolen from Binance, worth about $1.5 billion at the time of the theft, was split into 19 addresses on the day of the hack and has not moved since. The nine largest Ethereum addresses, holding 89% of the attacker's ETH, have not moved since October 30.

Most of the laundering so far, about $220 million in TRX, has run through Tron, where a single distribution wallet sent 684.6 million TRX to hundreds of new addresses over 51 hours, including through the night in Pyongyang. Almost all of it was bridged to Ethereum.

All of the stolen Solana, about $87 million at current prices, has been swapped, bridged or mixed, and the attacker has started laundering the stolen XRP, with about $50 million converted so far and $226 million remaining. In total, about $124 million has been converted into Bitcoin, much of it through cross-chain service THORChain.

The speed has fueled speculation that AI agents are moving the funds, but Elliptic said the evidence points to conventional automation. "The laundering is clearly automated, but nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions," the firm said.

Elliptic expects the attacker to swap the ETH for Bitcoin and then cash out through OTC brokers and exchanges over a period of months. After the Bybit hack, North Korea laundered all 499,000 stolen ETH within 10 days but took months to cash out, the firm noted.

Binance has continued to restore services. On Friday, the exchange said users could now verify that their balances are backed at least 1:1 using the zero-knowledge proof for its latest proof-of-reserves snapshot, and that it aims to reopen withdrawals for all tokens on restored networks by the end of next week.

#5107. American Bitcoin’s $126M profit masks mining pressure as rivals turn to AI

The Eric Trump-backed miner reported $126 million in quarterly net income, largely driven by gains on its Bitcoin holdings, despite rising mining costs and shrinking margins.

American Bitcoin reported a quarterly net income of $126 million on Wednesday, but rising mining costs and falling profits from mining raise questions about its long-term strategy as major rivals pivot towards artificial intelligence.

The Eric Trump-backed company’s profit was driven by a $197.3 million noncash gain on its Bitcoin (BTC) holdings. Meanwhile, mining revenue fell 5.5% to $63.4 million from the previous quarter, while the cost to mine one BTC rose about 15% to $42,100.

Excluding gains from its Bitcoin holdings, American Bitcoin would have reported an operating loss of approximately $5.5 million, according to its financial statements. The company also recorded a separate $64.3 million loss on derivatives.

“Despite seasonal power cost pressures, we continued mining Bitcoin at a structural discount to spot,” American Bitcoin CEO Mike Ho said in Wednesday’s earnings announcement.

Earnings per share stood at $1.73, beating analysts’ consensus estimate of $0.26, according to Yahoo Finance data.

Bitcoin was trading at $76,119, down 5% over the past 24 hours as of 8:50 p.m. UTC, according to CoinGecko data. Shares of American Bitcoin (ABTC), meanwhile, closed Thursday down about 15% at $5.31.

The results highlight an interesting divide among Bitcoin miners: while American Bitcoin continues mining and holding BTC, competitors like Riot Platforms, MARA Holdings and Core Scientific are investing in AI to find new sources of revenue.

David Gamble, founder of asset management company Enigma Holdings, which focuses on Bitcoin mining and AI infrastructure, said American Bitcoin’s reported profit does not tell the full story of its mining business.

“Ultimately, mining Bitcoin below its market price doesn’t automatically mean you’re creating shareholder value,” Gamble told Cointelegraph.

American Bitcoin’s gross mining profit fell to $25.8 million from $33 million in the previous quarter. Its reported mining margin dropped to 41% from 49%, leaving the company with a smaller share of mining revenue after direct costs.

The company’s $63.4 million in quarterly revenue also fell short of analysts’ estimates of $74.35 million, according to Yahoo Finance data, a gap of nearly 15%.

The company mined 891 BTC during the quarter, down from 932 BTC in the previous three months. It also said higher electricity prices were the main reason mining costs increased.

“I don’t necessarily think their accumulation strategy is flawed, but I do think it raises questions about long-term sustainability,” Gamble said, pointing to increasing mining difficulty and the need to keep investing in newer and more efficient technology to compete.

Compared with the same period last year, revenue was nearly unchanged, falling from $64.2 million to $63.4 million. Reported profit, however, jumped from $3.5 million to $126 million, largely reflecting gains on the company’s Bitcoin holdings.

Related: Bitcoin miners face widening AI financing costs as lenders scrutinize deals

American Bitcoin’s strategy comes as competitors like Riot Platforms, MARA Holdings and Core Scientific rapidly expand into AI.

Riot is building large AI data centers, according to its latest earnings. Meanwhile MARA is expanding into AI while continuing to mine Bitcoin. Core Scientific, on the other hand, has already shifted much of its business toward AI data centers.

American Bitcoin, by contrast, continues building its BTC reserves, holding 8,427 BTC at the end of September, up from 3,418 BTC a year earlier.

“The real competitive advantage in this industry is shifting toward energy infrastructure,” Gamble said. “It’s no longer just about how much Bitcoin you can mine or how much hashrate you can deploy.”

Gamble also explained that Bitcoin miners and AI companies are competing for the same power supply, and AI data centers could make more money than Bitcoin mining in some areas.

“That doesn’t mean every mining facility can simply transition into AI,” he said. “You still have to account for depreciation, equipment replacement, financing costs, and the opportunity cost of deploying that capital elsewhere.”

#5106. 7 Pendant Lights to Perfect Your Overhead Lighting Scheme (2026)

Sure, recessed lighting serves a functional purpose, but pendant lights are where things get interesting. A quick sweep through any home tour, and you’ll recognize the strategically placed Louis Poulsen above the kitchen island, an Isamu Noguchi in the entryway, and a Herman Miller saucer pendant hovering over the living room that will steal the show. Below are seven pendant light fixtures that set the mood instead of harshening the vibe. We rounded up the best pendant lights for various design personalities, including a vintage-inspired showpiece and a few variations of low-key ambient lighting. Among them are most of the different types of pendant lights you’re likely to come across, with picks for modern minimalists, Belle Époque enthusiasts, and everyone in between.

First developed in 1958, the PH 5 is a classic for a reason. Renowned Danish lighting manufacturer Louis Poulsen designed the iconic pendant with a three-shade system to equally distribute light. While the pendant works for that functional purpose, it also doubles as an unconventional art piece. Choose from a number of monochromatic and multitone colorways, including this steel blue.

Look familiar? That’s because this large pendant, inspired by Noguchi’s Akaris, hangs in many an AD home tour, including above the dining room table in this Japandi-inspired Bay Area home and in the bedroom of this South African–inspired craft home, for example. There are a few reasons why this style of paper lighting is so popular: It goes with everything; it’s affordable; and it adds a sense of levity to the room with its ethereal shape and its soft, warm glow. As lantern pendants go, this option from Design Within Reach is about as low-commitment as they come. It works anywhere you want ambient lighting rather than a spotlight.

Designer pendant lights can cost upwards of $500, but you can pay a fraction of that with finds from Article, a direct-to-consumer furniture store that sells high-quality wares at more palatable prices. That’s certainly the case with the Aino pendant, with matte colorways and a simple angular silhouette that illuminates whatever you’re chef-ing up in your Scandi-style kitchen. The metal cone shade and braided fabric cord make it an easy pick for task lighting over a counter, and if you’re working with a tighter kitchen space, there’s also a mini pendant version for $99 that ships with an LED bulb.

This three-tiered ceiling light from West Elm adds visual texture to blank space, making it a welcome addition to transitional areas in need of personality. A piece like this can also help to add structure to an open plan where the kitchen and dining rooms collide. This piece comes in 26-inch and 32-inch sizes (the larger is a proper large pendant light that can hold its own over a dining table), as well as in hardwired and plug-in options, making it one of the few statement hanging lights you can install without calling an electrician.

Any cold kitchen space dominated by stone and steel fixtures could use a touch of handmade whimsy. Hanging this handwoven rattan pendant over a kitchen island promises to warm up the whole room. Usually, two or three pendant lights hang over a kitchen island; this single shade provides a cozy, welcoming alternative, softening the light rather than spotlighting the countertop, so your mise en place won’t feel so interrogated.

This midcentury Herman Miller pendant, aptly named a saucer for its elliptical shape, is prized by commerce writer Julia Harrison for its retro, Mad Men–esque appeal. Despite the resemblance to our Hay pick above, Miller wasn’t riffing on Noguchi with this pendant: He wanted a Swedish silk-covered pendant he couldn’t justify buying, so he made his own using a sprayed plastic skin originally developed for mothballing Navy ships (whereas the orbital Hay version uses rice paper). The vertical ribbing gives it a shape that seems to be almost expanding as it hangs. Its white shade and chrome hardware make it easily adaptable to many (if not most) design styles.

This lighting fixture brings to mind 10-foot ceilings, Flemish tapestries, and an echoey marble entryway. If you’re lucky enough to have all of the above, this is exactly the sort of pendant your space needs. If not, luckily, the fixture carries that whole vibe on its own, with its cream glass bowl, brass detailing, and a sculpted metal tassel that adds a bit of movement. A vintage-inspired piece like this transforms a more humble hallway into something dignified and scenic.

#5104. The Best Fundamental Kettlebell Exercises for Beginners to Master

These are the cornerstone movements you need to train with these useful gym tools.

SOME OF THE most powerful, effectivekettlebell exercises rely on a few basic movements. Follow our practice recommendations to familiarize yourself with these building blocks, and they’ll become second nature in no time. Once you’ve mastered these, you’ll be able to tackle the more complex stuff—and supersize your gains.

These are the types of movements you'll learn in No Gym Required: Kettlebells, an MH training guide. You'll learn everything from the fundamentals for beginners to more advanced techniques and workout plans.

Want to get a jumpstart on your kettlebell practice? Men's Health MVP Premium members have exclusive access to a 14-day training program straight from the book, so you can kick off your workouts with expert guidance. Here's how you can get it.

Why: This move teaches you the hip hinge, which is an essential movement pattern that’s instrumental in making you powerful—and just may help you avoid lower back pain too. You should hinge whenever you bend to pick something up from the floor, pushing your butt back while keeping your lower back flat and your torso rigid. This protects your lower back while also setting you up to transfer force efficiently from the floor to your upper body. It’s key in kettlebell training: Whenever you deadlift, clean, snatch, squat, or row correctly, you’re also hinging. Take it slowly as you learn it. Later, you’ll be able to generate maximum power whether you’re holding a kettlebell, swinging a golf club, or punching a heavy bag.

Key Performance Points: Bend at the waist and not at the knees. Instead of visualizing your chest going down and up, focus on pushing your butt backward, then bring it forward. The closer you can keep the kettlebell to your body, the more effective these moves will be.

Sets and Reps: Do 3 sets of 10 to 12 reps. Focus on feeling your glutes. Rest 60 seconds between each set.

Why: The swing is one of the most popular and effective kettlebell moves, so mastering the finer points of the form is a must for anyone looking to add the implement to their repertoire. The key here is making sure to emphasize the hip hinge—when you lower the weight down, be sure not to squat before thrusting to snap the weight up. The hips are among your body’s most powerful joints. They’re also among the most mobile. Learning to contract your glutes and extend your hip joints forcefully is foundational to just about any athletic movement you can think of, and it all starts with the swing.

Key Performance Point: Don’t use your shoulders to drive the bell upward. Think of your arms like rope—they just attach your body to the bell. Your legs will create the power to swing upward. Focus on glute drive. The faster and more powerfully you stand and squeeze your glutes, the more easily the bell will swing upward. Keep your abs super-tight. That will protect your spine as the bell swings backward on each rep.

Sets and Reps: Aim to do 10 to 15 reps in 45 seconds, then rest for 15 seconds. Repeat for 5 sets.

Why: What happens when you combine a hip hinge and a front rack? It’s a little more complicated than just putting one and two together, but you’ll finish with a kettlebell clean. As we mentioned above, the hinge transfers power from the floor to your upper body with maximal efficiency. Cleaning the kettlebell is the next logical step: After you propel the bell upward with your glutes and hamstrings, your shoulder and forearm take over as you roll your wrist around and catch the bell in the rack position. Back in the days of the old-time strongmen, a lift wasn’t complete unless you moved a heavy object from the floor to overhead. The clean gets you most of the way there.

Key Performance Points: Thumb to chest. If you feel the kettlebell slamming into you, guide it more. Try a drill: Stand facing a wall and perform cleans. This will remind you not to swing the kettlebell out in front of you. Keep your thumb pointed in. This facilitates the roll around the wrist of the kettlebell into the rack position to finish the clean.

Why: The kettlebell rack position isn’t quite a standalone exercise either—but it’s an essential technique for holding the implement for all types of exercises, from lunges to carries. Mastering the rack position is fundamental to other kettlebell moves because it forces you to control and stabilize the weight in many directions at once. Taming the kettlebell in this way develops the grip, core, and glute strength you’ll need when you start pressing, swinging, and cleaning these unruly implements as the workouts grow in complexity.

Key Performance Points: The interior portion of the forearm should be pressed against your chest, which solves incorrect elbow position (it should be pointing down). Do not straighten your wrist. It should be in a strong flexed position, which will take pressure off your elbow. Keep your shoulders packed down. Your shoulder blades should be pulled down and back as opposed to lifted up like you’re shrugging (which will overtax the upper trap).

Sets and Reps: Start with single-arm front racks, doing each arm individually. Clean the bell into place, and hold for 30 seconds. Do 3 sets per side.

Why: Here, you’re working to push the weight overhead. Pressing a kettlebell is fundamental and you can start by doing it from a standing position. Learning to properly overhead press the bell does a few things. First off, it teaches you to own the overhead press position, which will set you up for overhead carries, windmills, getups, and other moves that will make kettlebell training even more fun. It also builds shoulder stability and increases your core stability.

Key Performance Points: Start from a good rack position. Keep your wrist forward at the top. Keep your biceps close to your ear, and keep a solid foundation by flexing your lats and shoulder blades.

Sets and Reps: Do 3 sets of 8 to 10 reps per side. Rest minimally between each set.

Why: Finally, you’ll use a hip hinge to bring the kettlebell overhead. This is the biggest test of your ability to generate power from the floor, and to coordinate the action of your upper and lower body. Learn to do it well and other strength and athletic movements will feel easy by comparison.

Key Performance Points: Don’t swing the kettlebell too far out in front of you. Guide the kettlebell even closer to your body and higher with your high pull. Once it’s level with your head, you’re done pulling—now you’re punching, getting your hand up to that locked-out position.

Sets and Reps: Do 10 reps, switch arms, and repeat.

Why: The basic halo is an incredibly effective movement that builds shoulder stability while working the core. It strengthens your mind-muscle connection, requiring full concentration and a team effort from your shoulders, triceps, back, and core to maneuver the unwieldy weight around your head. As a result, you can increase focus, boost intensity, and get the most out of every single rep. There’s more. In most gym movements, the weight follows a linear path. The halo is what exercise physiologists call a “transverse plane”—or rotational—exercise, which forces you to initiate movement and stabilize all 360 degrees of your core muscles, much as you do in life and sport. Over time, transverse plane moves build a tougher, more resilient core that is able to withstand shocks and absorb impact from all directions.

Key Performance Points: Keep your abs tight! The halo is harder than you think, and very easily can become a hula dance, with your hips moving and your lower back arching. Offset this by tightening your abs to pull your ribcage in. Don’t rush. Work the bell around your head slowly.

Sets and Reps: Try the exercise for 10 to 20 reps per direction to start before adding extra challenges, like the kneeling position or a squat, for a bigger metabolic impact.

Why: This compound exercise isn't just for the barbell. You'll hone your hip hinge using a kettlebell to deadlift, which can be a great reason for a beginner to add the movement to their routine. You're working the same movement as the good morning—you'll just change the position of the load to pick it up from the floor (which winds up being much more practical, both in your training and IRL).

Key Performance Points: Don't look up. As you lift the weight from the floor, keep your gaze trained on a spot on the floor just in front of you. This will help to keep your neck in a neutral position, which is important for spine health.

Sets and Reps: Start with 8 to 12 reps. You won't be able to load up as heavily with kettlebells as other implements, so focus on mastering the movement pattern here.

Why: This exercise is one of the best ways for beginners to learn how to squat, period. The position of the load helps to reinforce proper posture without being too difficult to handle, reinforcing bracing and spinal stability in ways the back squat doesn't.

Key Performance Points: Don't sweat depth. You'll be in a good position to lower down to the right spot for you; just avoid stopping before you hit parallel.

Sets and Reps: Start with 8 to 12 reps. Like the deadlift, you won't be able to load up as heavily with kettlebells as other implements when you squat, so focus on mastering the movement pattern here.

Why: This might look like you're just walking in place, but the kettlebell march is actually a challenging core exercise. The key is in the position of the load and bracing your abs—which you'll quickly discover is much harder than it looks.

Key Performance Points: Don't rush. Your steps should be slow and deliberate as you fight to keep your hips square and the weight level.

Sets and Reps: You can march for reps or for time. Try for 30 seconds on, 30 seconds off for 3 minutes.

Why: You’re giving your abs a uniquely challenging movement here, addressing both the bracing and rotational aspects of your core. If that weren’t enough, holding the weight overhead is a sneaky-effective way to hone shoulder health, too.

Key Performance Points: Key your eyes locked on the weight overhead throughout the movement. Keep your ribcage tight, and move slowly with intention instead of jerking up and down.

Sets and Reps: Perform 3 sets of 6 to 8 reps per side, making sure to move slowly through each step.

Why: This is your opportunity to build power without as much technical challenge as the snatch. Even better, once you swap kettlebells for the barbell lots of people use for push presses, you’ll be putting your shoulders in a much healthier position.

Key Performance Points: You’re doing a push press, not a thruster. Don’t squat down; keep your depth shallow.

#5103. Core Scientific to quit Bitcoin mining by year-end as AI business surges

The former Bitcoin mining heavyweight plans to become a pure-play data center provider in 2027, with mining now accounting for just 7.5% of quarterly revenue.

Core Scientific said it expects to exit Bitcoin mining by the end of 2026 as it shifts its business toward artificial intelligence and high-performance computing infrastructure.

“We expect to complete the wind-down during the fourth quarter and enter 2027 as a pure-play, high-density colocation provider,” said Core Scientific chief financial officer Jim Nygaard during a Q3 earnings call on Thursday.

Core Scientific had previously disclosed in a July quarterly filing that hosted mining would end by Dec. 31 as it continued to wind down its self-mining operations. The comment establishes a targeted completion date for its complete Bitcoin mining exit.

Its latest quarterly results show how far the business has shifted. Third-quarter self-mining revenue fell to $12.3 million from $57.4 million a year earlier, while revenue from hosting other miners dropped to $5.2 million from $8.7 million.

Bitcoin mining generated just $17.5 million, or 7.5%, of Core Scientific’s $232.9 million in third-quarter revenue, compared with $215.4 million from colocation services.

The company had nearly 50% fewer miners online at the end of September than at the end of 2025, according to Nygaard.

Core Scientific’s move into AI hosting took shape in 2024 after signing an initial hosting agreement with CoreWeave in March that year, followed by a much larger deal announced in June.

At the time, Core Scientific presented AI hosting as an expansion alongside its mining business. Its June 2024 announcement emphasized preserving Bitcoin mining capacity.

By December 2025, The Wall Street Journal reported that Core Scientific was already targeting to wind down its Bitcoin mining operations by 2028.

Related: American Bitcoin’s $126M profit masks mining pressure as rivals turn to AI

Mining economics added pressure in the months following.

Core Scientific recorded $266.5 million in mining-related asset impairments in the first quarter of 2026, citing weaker Bitcoin prices, historically low hashprice and falling digital asset mining equipment values in the secondary market.

During the second quarter, it terminated its mining equipment agreement with Block and Proto, recording a $41.9 million loss as part of the transition.

The planned exit comes as the US election results introduce fresh uncertainty for companies expanding AI infrastructure.

Opponents of data-center development were among the winners of the Democrats’ sweeping midterm gains, Reuters reported Wednesday.

The backlash centers partly on electricity bills and the demands large facilities place on local resources.

Cointelegraph reached out to Core Scientific for comment.

#5101. Anthropic IPO Valuation Could Be Slashed in Half to $1T After AI Stock Rout: Report

Anthropic IPO Valuation Could Be Slashed in Half to $1T After AI Stock Rout: Report

Major investors are increasingly doubtful that Anthropic's initial public offering can go ahead at its expected valuation of $1.5 trillion, after a sharp sell-off in AI stocks, The Wall Street Journal reported on Thursday.

Anthropic, which filed last month to list on Nasdaq under the ticker "ANT," has been aiming to go public the week before Thanksgiving and raise up to $100 billion, according to the Journal. But three major investors who plan to take part in the IPO told the paper the valuation will likely need to come down, with one suggesting it could be closer to $1 trillion.

The company could also raise less than planned, perhaps around $80 billion, one investor told the Journal. Another wondered whether Anthropic might delay the offering until market conditions improve. Anthropic executives and advisers are expected to meet in the coming days to discuss the plans, according to the report.

The doubts follow a rout in AI stocks on Thursday, as investors weighed whether Democratic gains in the midterms could bring stricter oversight of data center construction. The S&P 500 closed down 4.4% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq 100 fell 6.3%, both their worst days since April 2025, while the Philadelphia semiconductor index slid 12.3% and Nvidia dropped 9.2%, NBC News reported. Stocks partly recovered on Friday morning: as of 11:21 a.m. ET, the Nasdaq Composite was up 1.4% and Nvidia had gained 3.4%, according to Yahoo Finance.

Democrats are planning bills to shield consumers from rising electricity costs tied to data centers, require independent testing of advanced AI systems and clarify when AI companies can be held liable for harm, Reuters reported, though any legislation would likely face steep odds while Trump remains in the White House. The party also plans closer scrutiny of leading AI companies, including Anthropic, according to the report.

Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-VT) has called for a "national data center moratorium," while Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has pledged to pause data center approvals in a state that is home to more than a quarter of planned U.S. data center capacity, CNBC reported.

A delay to Anthropic's IPO is now an "escalating risk" in the current environment, technology analyst Richard Windsor told The Telegraph. "A postponement would protect against near-term valuation risks and would be more likely if the correction sustains over a longer period," said Nalin Patel, an analyst at PitchBook, according to the paper.

Some investors want to see Anthropic's October results before assuming a cut. The company's annualized revenue run rate topped $80 billion at the end of October, and it had $146.6 billion in cash as of September 30, according to the Journal.

Traders have pushed back their expectations. On Polymarket, the odds that Anthropic lists by November 20 stood at 43% on Friday, while the chance of a listing by the end of the year was 71%. On Kalshi, where traders bet on when Anthropic will officially confirm its IPO, the odds of that happening before December 1 fell 15 points to 59%, while the chance of it happening before January 1 slipped to 68%. On Decrypt Predict, a prediction market developed by Decrypt's parent company, Dastan, traders also put the odds of an Anthropic IPO before 2027 at 71%.

The valuation now in question is already well below earlier expectations. In September, Decrypt reported that a leaked draft filing showed Anthropic was seeking a valuation of around $2 trillion.

This article was drafted with the assistance of Claude, an AI model developed by Anthropic.

#5100. What Is It Supposed to Feel Like to Push Your Limits? 5-Time CrossFit Games Champ Mat Fraser Knows.

No pain, no gain? The GOAT shares his perspective.

MAT FRASER UNDERSTANDS what it’s like to push your limits. But the five-time CrossFit Games champ found the motivation to train to failure at the 2015 CrossFit Games, when he finished in second place. Fraser remembers feeling devastated—finishing as a runner-up felt like a personal offense to his purpose and identity.

“Once I made that commitment to get back in the gym and give it another shot, I kept that moment fresh,” Fraser says in the latest episode of Strong Talk. “Knowing that you don't get that beautiful moment in front of thousands of people without the thousands of moments by yourself in your gym when no one's patting you on the back and no one's cheering for you.”

What did it feel like to push his limits in his workouts? Fraser says he’s on the right track when he’s “tasting pennies,” the top of his head is tingling, and his heart rate is over 200 bpm. “Leave no doubt on the table, and be proud of myself. Then no matter what results I get, I know that's what I earned,” he explains.

But despite his preparation, Fraser is only human. Despite his impressive accolades, he never got rid of the pre-event jitters. Fraser tells MH fitness director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S. and exercise physiologist and strength coach Dr. Pat Davidson that he used to dry heave or grab a bucket as his breakfast came back up before every competition. In the episode, the trio delved more into the hardest workouts he did to prepare for the Games, what it's like to train recreationally versus for competition, and how to exercise and fuel your body when you only have 15 minutes.

Want more deep-dive fitness wisdom from Samuel and other celebs and experts who've been on our Strong Talk podcast? Check out all our episodes here.

#5098. Crypto exchanges tout cold storage ratios after Binance attack

Coinbase says it keeps more than 99% of customer assets offline, while Gate reports 88.26% of its total assets are kept in cold storage.

Crypto exchanges were quick to reassure users following the attack on Binance’s hot wallet infrastructure last week, with several companies disclosing how they divide assets between cold storage and internet-connected hot wallets this week.

Crypto exchange Gate on Thursday launched a “Proof of Security” (PoSEC) framework, reporting that 88.26% of its total assets were in cold storage and 11.74% in hot wallets. It claims its cold storage could cover 104.58% of customer assets even if its hot wallets were completely drained.

“By publicly disclosing the distribution of assets between platform cold storage and hot wallets, PoSEC provides visibility into asset storage structures and their ability to cover potential losses under extreme scenarios,” Gate said. It noted the data is still undergoing independent review by a third-party audit firm.

Threat actors attacked Binance’s hot-wallet infrastructure last week, stealing approximately $6.36 billion, most of it in about 20 minutes, according to TRM Labs. Binance CEO Richard Teng said the “vast majority” of user assets are held in cold wallets, but the company has not provided a precise split.

Other crypto exchanges have been quick to share their cold storage ratios this week.

In a blog post Wednesday, Coinbase said that more than 99% of customer assets are held in offline cold storage and that it continuously rebalances excess assets to cold storage.

“Binance’s reported hot wallet incident has understandably left customers asking how Coinbase protects customer assets from something similar happening here,” it said.

On Tuesday, Upbit, the largest cryptocurrency exchange in South Korea, confirmed it holds 99.17% of its assets in cold wallets, with the remaining 0.83% in hot wallets as of September 2026. These figures are already disclosed to the Financial Supervisory Service.

“This represents the highest cold wallet storage ratio among South Korea’s five major KRW-based cryptocurrency exchanges and is substantially above the regulatory minimum of 80%,” a spokesperson for Upbit parent company Dunamu told Cointelegraph, in response to questions about South Korea’s call for crypto exchanges to increase their cold storage ratios.

“Our aim is to keep our hot wallet holdings at an absolute minimum, with a target of maintaining them below 1% of customer digital assets,” they added.

Related: Fed, OCC check US banks after Binance hack as experts flag security risks

Other major South Korean exchanges have reported lower cold-wallet shares. Financial Supervisory Service figures cited in earlier reporting put Bithumb at 94.76%, Coinone at 85%, Digital X, formerly Korbit, at 82.79%, and GOPAX at 80.54% at the end of September.

OKX’s existing security overview, last updated on Sept. 18, 2026, said it holds more than 95% of client crypto assets in cold wallets and less than 5% in hot wallets.

The reported ratios are not necessarily directly comparable because exchanges use different reporting periods and may calculate cold-storage shares against different asset bases.

Cold storage ratios, however, offer only part of the picture of an exchange’s security.

Offline wallets can still be exposed through compromised signing systems, inadequate access controls or employees approving fraudulent transactions.

Hong Kong’s Securities and Futures Commission warned in an August 2025 circular that such weaknesses could compromise both hot- and cold-wallet infrastructure.

Magazine: Asian crypto exchanges batten down the hatches as hacks surge: Asia Express

#5097. 8 Best Online Framing Services for Displaying Art (2026)

Sometimes the most daunting part of getting art on your walls isn't tracking down a cool piece of art but finding the right frame for it. Generic online options tend to look cheap, and they’re offered in only the most basic dimensions. On the other end of the spectrum, a custom framer might quote you a higher price than the actual piece you’re framing. So, what’s an art lover to do? Luckily, there are a few tried and tested online companies that offer art gallery caliber frames at reasonable prices. Many take care of everything from printing to framing, with perks like complimentary hanging hardware to sweeten the deal. So if you’re ready to rescue those art prints still rolled up in a tube in your closet, scroll on to discover the online framing resources we trust with our precious works.

Framebridge’s offerings are more stylized than a lot of the other options you’ll find online, which is why it’s our top pick. Material options range from gilded bamboo to distressed whitewashed wood, with all frames supplied with matting, UV-protected acrylic, and hanging supplies. Either send in your artwork or upload a photograph for Framebridge to print at the desired size. “I'd been holding onto a few posters—a slick Valentino one and some vintage designs from a place in Tribeca I’m gatekeeping—when one of the brand’s numerous ads posed the answer to my framing woes,” says shopping writer Julia Harrison, who took her items to one of the brand’s physical locations (there are 44 across the country). After being walked through a different range of options and a month-long wait, Harrison told us the results were worth it. “I ended up ‘floating’ each piece I brought in—simple black frames with white or off-white backdrops for the first two, and a tortoise frame with gilded matte backing for the Robert Altman poster I brought in. I'm delighted with the final product—I feel like I'm, for once, treating my things respectfully.” The company can also frame objects such as commemorative buttons or sports jerseys that are less than 3/8" deep.

Your local craft store might be the obvious first stop for framing supplies, but that doesn't mean you have to take the DIY route. AD teamed up with Michaels, North America's largest framer, on an exclusive collection of five custom frame styles: Vintage Luxury, Accessible Contemporary, Artisanal Layers, Eclectic Maximalist, and Space-Age Modernism. Despite its big-box reputation, each frame is made to order and comes with acid-free matting, UV-protective acrylic, and hanging hardware—so it's ready to display as soon as it arrives. The collection is available through Michaels Custom Framing, with options to design your frame online or in-store. There's a frame to suit your art (and your decorating style) with options like ornate, gilded profiles and more contemporary woodgrain finishes.

Artifact Uprising will help you personalize your space with prints framed in a variety of modern styles, from floating acrylic and brass wall frames to wood tabletop frames. Each of the streamlined frames comes with hardware and hanging instructions to make it look like you worked with a professional galleryist. In fact, the brand also offers helpful guides like gallery wall decorating tips and an array of free, downloadable art to make the process feel less daunting. In addition to traditional framing, the brand’s non-frame art display options include wood photo ledges and photo hangers.

Anyone looking to create physical mementos from their digital libraries should check out Art.com, which specializes in converting digital images into works of art with just a few clicks. Options include traditional frames (with over 50 styles to choose from), canvas prints, wood mounts, and framed canvases. Customers even have access to select photo editing tools should they be looking to touch up their selections and the site will also save your images for up to three months should you be on the fence about final selects.

The framing solutions at Level Frames are handcrafted from sustainably harvested wood and environmentally friendly shellacs. Users can upload an image to print and then choose between a black or white mat and from three types of glazing. If you’re unsure of the best proportions to opt for, with a click of the golden-ratio button the site suggests the right frame based on the size of your artwork. Like Framebridge, posters and prints can also be sent to Level's studio for framing. (Note to music fans: The site specializes in framing vinyl records.)

You might not have thought it, but Etsy is one of the most affordable solutions we’ve found for custom framing. Many sellers on the site allow you to digitally send in the image you’d like to frame and the finished product will ship directly to your doorstep. If you’re looking to frame a piece of artwork or a poster you already own, many storefronts specialize in creating custom frames that will pair perfectly with your piece (just make sure your measurements are as accurate as can be).

Framers prove they know their stuff when the first question is exactly what you’re framing. Simply Framed starts with that—is it a canvas? An art print? A scarf? Next you can browse more than 35 frame choices, including sleek gallery styles or a shimmering gold leaf that can accommodate works up to 40 by 60 inches. The company can even create a shadowbox or stretch a canvas within a frame and will recommend different options depending on the medium and size of the piece. Once you’ve placed your order, you’ll receive a tube with a prepaid return label. Pop the artwork in the mail, and a few days later it should show up, framed, on your doorstep.

5” x 5” to 40” x 50” (or up to 80" unassembled)

American Frame has been in the business since 1973 and offers over 400 wood and metal frame options. The company will also print artwork and photos on a selection of nine paper types, canvas, Plexiglas, or aluminum. Customers can choose from a large variety of frames and mats and take advantage of retouching, mounting, and glazing options.

#5095. One of Fall's Best New Colognes Was Inspired by... Gary, Indiana?

Fulton & Roark Cathedral of Ruins is a dark, sophisticated scent that’s perfect for the season.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

AS SOMEONE WHO spent large chunks of his early twenties driving around the country pretending to be Jack Kerouac (while acting more like Hunter S. Thompson), I’ve spent time in just about every contiguous U.S. state. Some, as you might imagine, are more interesting than others—and Indiana isn’t exactly topping the list. So when I discovered one of my new favorite fall colognes was inspired by Gary, Indiana, I began questioning my own reality. But Fulton & Roark Cathedral of Ruins Extrait de Parfumis that good.

The only thing I know about Gary, Indiana is that it’s where Michael Jackson and the Jackson 5 were from—and they left. But after reading about the inspiration behind Fulton & Roark’s latest scent—and, more importantly, smelling it—I’m wondering if it might be worth a trip. Specifically, the fragrance is inspired by City Methodist Church in Gary, Indiana.

Built in the 1920s, the church is still admired for its grand Gothic architecture. Over the years, its roof caved in, but the stone walls remained intact, allowing vegetation to grow throughout its halls. Equal parts creepy and beautiful, it’s exactly the type of place I’d like to sneak into to see if I could spend a night without freaking out.

More so, its mysterious, earthy, and intriguing yet inviting atmosphere captures exactly the type of vibe I look for in cologne this time of year. After wearing the fragrance for over a month, here’s how it lived up to its inspiration.

Cathedral of Ruins opens with an intense combination of cypress, black peppercorn, and rose, before heart notes of saffron and Peru balsam begin to emerge. After 20 minutes or so, the base notes of leather and frankincense really start to take hold. The result is a woody amber scent that feels lived in and worldly, like cracking open an old copy of The Portrait of Dorian Gray you found at the back of the library.

It’s sophisticated without being pretentious, and noticeable without being loud or overpowering. After applying two to three sprays to the neck and one to my wrists, the cologne maintains most of its strength for seven to eight hours, but lingers much longer. My girlfriend has noticed whiffs of it on my skin as much as 12 hours after applying it.

SHOP FULTON & ROARK CATHEDRAL OF RUINS EXTRAIT DE PARFUM

As someone who was kicked out of his church in middle school (story for another time), I’m not exactly what you’d call a man of faith. However, I am a fan of historic architecture, and that’s something Cathedral of Ruins nails. The combination of frankincense, saffron, and leather gives this fragrance a rustic, sophisticated feel reminiscent of a musky, old library or chic used bookstore in Upstate New York.

There is also, of course, a Gothic backdrop to the scent. I tend to get sad at the beginning of November, as it’s no longer socially acceptable to talk extensively about horror movies throughout the day now that Halloween is over. This cologne is ideal for those of us who know spooky season never ends.

It’s mysterious, alluring, and deep while still being approachable. It’s subtle enough for the office yet interesting enough for the bar. More than anything, it’s perfect for fall, capturing the beauty of falling leaves and cooling temperatures.

Cathedral of Ruins is for the guy who knows spooky season is a year-long commitment. Its woody amber profile blends cypress, pepper, rose, saffron, leather, and frankincense into a dark, sophisticated scent that feels equally at home in a library, bar, or office. It lasts for most of the day while staying noticeable without becoming overwhelming, making it an easy cold-weather go-to.

#5094. Chainalysis says breach exposed password hashes and live tokens

Chainalysis disclosed password hashes and live tokens were exposed after initially reporting only non-sensitive customer data was accessed.

Chainalysis on Wednesday disclosed a wider impact from a cyberattack it initially described as involving only non-sensitive customer information.

“On Monday, we discovered certain customer information including names, usernames, password hashes and live tokens for all Chainalysis users were exposed to a malicious actor,” the blockchain analytics firm said in an update to its incident notice.

The company did not disclose whether each type of information was exposed for every user and declined to provide Cointelegraph with additional information beyond its published disclosure.

Chainalysis initially said on Oct. 30 that the intrusion exposed user IDs, names and email addresses but found no evidence that attackers accessed authentication credentials or session tokens.

Another Wednesday update stated an “on-path attack” exposed some customers’ passwords. The company said it had contained and remediated that attack vector and was locking affected accounts and requiring password resets.

Despite the broader exposure, Chainalysis said it still had no evidence that attackers accessed investigation data or manipulated its attribution data.

#5092. Crypto Liquidations Top $1 Billion as Bitcoin Tests 11-Week Low

Crypto Liquidations Top $1 Billion as Bitcoin Tests 11-Week Low

Bitcoin is trading near $75,570 on Friday after touching $74,780.91 today; its lowest intraday reading since August 21, 77 days earlier.

The crypto market retreat followed a brutal selloff in AI stocks and another round of forced closures in derivatives markets.

According to CoinGlass data, $1.05 billion in crypto have been liquidated over the preceding 24 hours. Long positions, which profit when prices rise, accounted for $930.04 million, or 88.2%, compared with $124.69 million in shorts.

That 80%-20% gap in liquidations also matches the overall sentiment tracked by the Fear and Greed Index which is set at 28 points (approaching extreme fear) out of 100, where 0 is a state of maximum fear among traders, and 100 is maximum greed. For context, the index was at a neutral just a week ago, before the midterm elections.

Bitcoin accounted for $267.20 million of the total, with 88.9% from long positions, while Ethereum recorded $167.14 million, 81.1% of it long.

Liquidation happens when an exchange automatically closes a position because its collateral is no longer sufficient, sometimes adding selling pressure to an existing decline. Unlike normal trading, in which investors can handle any unrealized loss, trading with leverage gives a maneuver window that can end in the loss of all funds once an asset goes beyond or below a specific price.

The post-midterm election fear is spreading on Wall Street, and U.S. technology stocks took the first big hit on Thursday. The Nasdaq Composite fell 5.52%, its worst session in 19 months, while the Philadelphia semiconductor index plunged more than 12% and Nvidia alone lost over 9%.

Democratic victories in Tuesday's midterm elections raised concerns that tougher AI regulation could slow construction of the data centers behind this year's chip rally. The S&P 500 lost 4.36% on Thursday, erasing roughly $3 trillion in market value across its constituent companies.

Bitcoin had already given back its post-election relief rally as chipmakers sank, as Decrypt reported Thursday. Forced closures of bullish crypto positions accelerated the fall, though simultaneous moves in stocks and tokens doesn’t necessarily mean the equity rout alone caused Bitcoin's decline.

A stronger-than-expected U.S. October jobs report also increased expectations of another Federal Reserve rate hike in December. Higher borrowing costs can weigh on speculative markets, leaving crypto exposed even as chip shares recover.

Right now, Bitcoin trades near $75,570, with the current candle still forming after a low of $74,780.91. The weekly chart shows another red candle and a sequence of lower highs since September, when Bitcoin briefly traded above $87,000.

The 50-day exponential moving average, or EMA, gives more weight to recent prices and sits near $79,756, above the 200-day EMA around $76,497. That ordering remains bullish on a longer view, but Bitcoin is now trading below both averages, a sign the latest decline has overtaken the lagging bullish signal.

The Relative Strength Index, or RSI, reads 37.7, below the neutral 50 mark but still above the 30 level generally considered oversold. The Average Directional Index, or ADX, is at 19.7, typical of trendless states—but the negative directional indicator exceeds the positive one, pointing to weak but bearish directional pressure.

The immediate support is $74,780, the horizontal line and intraday low on the supplied chart. A daily close below it would shift attention toward the $73,000 to $72,500 area around August's breakout, followed by the psychological $70,000 level if that zone fails.

On a recovery, Bitcoin first faces the 200-day EMA near $76,497, then some weak and immediate near $80,000. Friday's daily candle is still open, with its $74,780.91 low the immediate level to defend.

The show may not be for everyone, but Spader, as has often been the case since the ’80s, is doing truly unbelievable work.

Spader plays the physical manifestation of Ultron—the proto-AI doombot he voiced in 2015’s Avengers: The Age of Ultron, who has now assumed the form of James Spader, sporting a feral salt-and-pepper beard, hexagonal specs, and defense against the dark arts professor loungewear. He is glorious, bloviating on subjects from cyberpunk philosophy to fatherhood in a scotch and cigar baritone with a hint of mischief dancing behind his eyes.

With each passing VisionQuest episode, Spader not only helps push the show’s knotty plotting forward, but chews on each bit of scenery with the best of them (he’s particularly effective when sharing the screen with T'Nia Miller, who plays another high-minded scientist-turned-synthetic being). Spader’s history as the magnetic degenerate who captivated Hollywood in the ’80s and ’90s is more than evident in each episode of Marvel’s latest series, especially if you know where to look for it.

Spader, now 66, started his career as a calculating piece of shit in Pretty in Pink, before hitting it big on the American indie film scene, starting in Steven Soderbergh’s smash hit Sex, Lies & Videotape as, what else? An unnervingly direct, impotent drifter who interviews women on camera about their sexual experiences then beats off to the footage. From there he had brushes with conventional stardom in kind of blockbusters like Stargate, or, trying to belie his lot in life, miscast as straight men to Rob Lowe or John Cusack, but through the ’90s he becomes the maestro of the psychosexual thriller, showing up as obsessive compulsive rapist werewolves, hitchhiking gamblers, hooker-banging lawyers, and widowers who can’t get their face out of Susan Sarandon’s crotch. When you work on projects like these—writing about the entirety of a successful working actor’s filmography—there’s often a staggering level of diversity between genres and roles. What’s staggering about Spader’s career is how little of anything else there is. It’s all delinquent sicko shit.

It is poetic that for the most part, at the turn of the century, Spader migrates to television right around the time the sex thriller goes out of style. He ages, and wins long overdue acting trophies, on the small screen. Perhaps most interestingly, Spader now may be as interesting a presence as he ever has been. Through the years, he’s gained weight and lost hair and his voice has deepened, but he continues to ooze libertine, alpha sexuality and idiosyncratic intensity.

With his return to Marvel as a face, as well as a voice, the time was right to clarify, in the parlance of basketball clip packages on YouTube, “James Spader was a problem,” in several senses of the word. People today might not realize there was a time a Hollywood star could be defined by playing, and presenting to the public as a seductive, creepy, sex pest weirdo, and there was a steady diet of projects that gave them opportunities to do that specific thing on screen. In Spader’s work specifically, we see a moment in American life and culture when Hollywood still had the guts, and the temerity, to be horny on main.

Spader has referred to himself as “Brat Pack Adjacent,” emerging with that cohort of young stars but never quite “of” them. His primary moment comes in this John Hughes classic as an asshole who wears a blazer to school with the sleeves cuffed. He might be the gold standard of a quintessential sniveling ‘80s high school bully this side of William Zabka.

It makes sense that near the beginning, then at the outset of the late period of his career, Spader faced off against Robert Downey Jr., because in a certain light Spader can be seen as Downey’s slightly misshapen clone. Both are twitchy, motor-mouthed wise-asses incapable of a straight line delivery, who contain deep and limitless reserves of quippy charisma, only Spader’s juice is slightly tainted. Downey is right on the line of smarmy and seductive, and Spader is just over it. He’s alluring but a little off-putting, a little annoying, a little frightening, and always compelling. In Less Than Zero you worry for Downey, lost in Bret Easton Ellis’s debauched Los Angeles Neverland. You don’t worry about Spader, who is right where he wants to be, operating with impunity and ruling comfortably in hell.

Spader’s first mature role is less “Spadery” than basically anything he'd done before or would do afterward, thanks in large part to the way he weaponizes his soft-spoken openness. He’s the only character in the film who is honest about who he is and what he wants in a seedy drama where everyone lies or elides, talking around their desires.

Young Roland Emmerich was aiming for a dumbass version of an ‘80s Spielberg space adventure, so for his version of Richard Dreyfuss, he chose shaggy, adorable, baby James Spader in an era when oddballs like Nic Cage could become action stars. Spader went in a different direction with his career, but we’ll always have this performance as a linguist/allergy-prone dweeb with a shoulder-length shag presenting ancient aliens as an academic argument, using hieroglyphics to unlock an artifact liquid portal that transports you across the universe. He’s perfectly paired with Kurt Russell, as two different phenotypes of asshole.

Ballard novel about people with a desire for sex wrapped in a desire for self-immolation, and Paul Haggis’s shallow, sappy, Oscar-winning exploration of race relations of the exact same title. There’s too much to say about this film to confine it to a blurb, but I’ll just mention that a mere 25 minutes into the film, Spader is having sex with America’s Sweetheart, Holly Hunter. At this point, she is the widow of a man who died in a car accident with Spader. They are in a car identical to the one that killed her husband, one that Spader has just purchased and nearly wrecked again, with both Spader and Hunter in the car. One of the most depraved films I’ve ever seen. Watch it immediately.

Before moving to TV, Spader had a wild turn of the century: He’s in a lot of duds that don’t really know what to do with him. In a way, it exposes what a unique and specific tool he is, making his long, prolific career even harder to wrap your head around—because we see how easily it can go wrong for him. One is a bizarre ghost rom-com with Michael Caine and Maggie Smith, another is a near unwatchable post-Seven thriller with an egregiously bad Keanu Reeves as the antagonist killer, and the last, an abhorrent sex comedy. It’s interesting to watch Spader fail, trying to fight against his nature and remake himself into something closer to a conventional leading man. Luckily, he found a medium that better understood how to channel his dark peculiarities.

Another fetish movie, this one based on a Mary Gaitskill short story. Or, as I like to think of it, “Straight Pillion.” Spader is perfectly measured here as a stammering, mean, tender dom that uses his OCD in a film about control and submission.

After a test run on The Practice, this is the beginning of James Spader: TV star, the dawn of the persona that will become his final form. The show may appear to be a conventional primetime legal drama, and sometimes it is, but it possesses deranged and horny mid-aughts sexual politics that would cause riots if it came on air today. This is because it feels tailored to Spader (and co-star William Shatner) and his various kinks and proclivities.

“There is no such thing as a product, don’t ever think there is. There is only sex. Everything is sex. You understand that what I’m telling you is a universal truth?” This is how much of a generation of Americans first meets Spader, as the enigmatic boss daddy Robert California, on an NBC sitcom that was averaging nearly seven million viewers per episode at the time. When Steve Carell exited The Office, Ed Helms stepped into his role in Dunder Mifflin’s office as well as the sitcom’s dynamic as goofy office manager. But Spader also came along, injecting something entirely different. As California, he’s a literal confidence man whose tieless collar is unbuttoned, his receding hair is tousled, and he holds a dark hypnotic power over the office—one not even John Krasinski’s cool-headed Jim is immune to. Throughout the show’s eighth season, the entire office competes for the favor of Spader’s expert power-dynamic manipulator, a soft reprisal of his role in Secretary.

After spending weeks delving into this strange, delightful filmography, it pains me to think there are many people out there of a certain age who will mainly think of Spader as “The Blacklist guy,” but that’s what 218 episodes and 10 years in a role will do to your public perception. Despite frequent notices it was filming in my neighborhood for many years, I never caught this show on air. Having screened a few for consideration, I now see it as both a step down, and a fulfillment of Spader’s whole career journey. On one hand, he’s something of a diet Hannibal Lecter, a mastermind criminal who turns himself over to the Feds to help take down criminals worse than he is (for reasons that aren’t initially clear). Over time, the show exposes its conventional, maudlin heart through made-for-TV beats. Spader is fully washed at this late date in life, and should be commended for not fighting that. Still, he’s always dominant, remaining a few steps ahead of his friends and foes, a high T individual with a working cock, using it frequently.

In other words, he remains a glorious deviant—to the bitter end.

#5086. Crypto Card Provider Gnosis Pay Says Hackers Accessed Customer Data

Crypto Card Provider Gnosis Pay Says Hackers Accessed Customer Data

Crypto card provider Gnosis Pay said Friday that hackers accessed customer information through the system behind its own branded cards, the latest in a string of security incidents across the industry.

The company’s monitoring systems detected unauthorized access on Nov. 3, and containment began within two hours, Gnosis Pay said in a blog post. It has since replaced the compromised security credentials and fixed the vulnerability. Customer funds weren’t affected, the company said.

The breach was limited to Gnosis Pay’s consumer business, whose platform was already scheduled for retirement on Dec. 20. Its enterprise platform, which powers white-label card programs for fintech firms, wallets and neobanks, wasn’t affected.

Gnosis Pay didn’t say how many customers were affected or what data was exposed, but said it emailed each affected customer with details of what was taken from their account.

“While funds were not affected, the security of personal information is just as important and we take full responsibility,” the company wrote on X.

Gnosis Pay said it has stepped up monitoring of card activity and wallets for fraud, and has made 3D Secure, an extra verification step, mandatory for all online card payments until the consumer platform is retired. It has reported the breach to its partners, its card issuer and data protection authorities, and is arranging specialist monitoring for signs that the data is misused.

We had a data breach affecting the system powering Gnosis Pay-branded cards (our B2C business line) that resulted in the exposure of certain customer information.

Everyone affected was sent an email today with full information.

We deeply regret that this happened. While funds…

— Gnosis Pay 💳 (@gnosispay) November 6, 2026

The company urged customers to be wary of unexpected messages about their cards, even ones that include real personal details or purchases. It said it will never ask for seed phrases, PINs or full card numbers, or ask users to move funds.

It also told customers to be careful about sharing their address or details of their crypto holdings, both online and offline.

That last warning reflects a growing threat. Physical “wrench attacks” on crypto holders, including home invasions and kidnappings, were on pace for a record year after more than $30 million was stolen in the first half of 2026, according to blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis.

The disclosure follows several other recent examples of breaches in crypto security. Beyond the record-setting $6.4 billion hack of the world’s largest crypto exchange in Binance, rival trading platform Coinbase said last week that a breach at analytics vendor Amplitude exposed some customer contact data.

Last month, hardware wallet maker Ledger said all confirmed cases in a suspected $87 million drain involved devices bought from CryptoBilis, a Southeast Asian reseller, and urged recent buyers to move their funds to new devices.

#5085. Hyperliquid Strategies seeks futures registration for perps

The HYPE treasury company spent $785 million on tokens during the quarter and wants to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the US.

A subsidiary of Nasdaq-listed HYPE treasury company Hyperliquid Strategies has applied to become a futures commission merchant (FCM), seeking to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the US.

“Owning HYPE is how we aim to capture economics associated with the Hyperliquid protocol; setting up an FCM is how we intend to help bring markets deployed on Hyperliquid onshore,” said Hyperliquid Strategies CEO David Schamis in the company’s results for the quarter ended Sept. 30, published on Thursday.

Hyperliquid’s HIP-3 framework allows independent operators to launch custom perpetual futures markets on the protocol’s onchain infrastructure by staking 500,000 HYPE tokens ($38.8 million). Proposed HIP-3 features would allow operators to create permissioned markets that restrict participation to approved addresses.

The subsidiary would handle US customer accounts for permissioned markets deployed on Hyperliquid, the company said. Both the FCM application and the proposed markets require regulatory approval.

Intercontinental Exchange, the parent company of the New York Stock Exchange, announced an agreement with Trade[XYZ] on Oct. 29 to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual markets to the US, subject to regulatory approvals. ICE plans to operate a dedicated market and clearing structure.

Related: Hyperliquid, Pump.fun account for nearly 90% of record $638M crypto buybacks: FT

During the quarter, Hyperliquid Strategies raised $911 million in equity capital. It spent $785 million to acquire 9.1 million HYPE and $17 million to repurchase 2.7 million PURR shares. The company held a total of 38.5 million HYPE as of Sept. 30, valued at $3.5 billion at the time.

“Hyperliquid is winning the parts of onchain finance that generate real fees, and nearly all of that value flows back to HYPE holders through programmatic buybacks,” Schamis said.

#5082. Is Your Penis Getting Smaller as You Age? This Common Factor Could Hide One to Two Inches.

IT PROBABLY SOUNDS like a nightmare scenario to think your penis is getting shorter. Maybe not even being visible at all. But it can happen as you age, and when it does, doctors refer to it as a buried penis.

Now it's not that your penis is physically getting shorter. It's just that most of it is hidden. More on that below.

CONVINCED YOU LOST an inch? Thankfully, your penis isn’t disappearing; it’s just hidden beneath fat tissue.

Justin Dubin, MD, urologist and men’s health specialist at Baptist Health, says that generally when you can’t see the penis, it’s usually because of low abdominal tissue surrounding the penis.

Essentially, your penis is still its regular size, but it doesn’t extend outside the body. Drogo Montague, MD, a urologist in Ohio tells MH what that looks like. “There is an opening where the penis would normally hang, and the head is usually flush with skin, but there’s no shaft outside of the body.”

Now you don’t just wake up one day with inches of your penis vanished. The effect occurs on a continuum, says Dr. Montague. You might notice at first, for instance, that your penis seems to get shorter and shorter. (Though, this is not the same as the small amount of shrinkage that some men see with age, which is due to skin cells around the penis becoming less elastic.)

“This is a serious condition because it will inhibit urination, intimacy, and personal hygiene,” says Scott Miller, MD, urologist and medical director of Wellstar Urology. “It can also strongly impact self-esteem and overall emotional health.”

MOST CASES OF buried penis are due to one thing: obesity.

While the rates of buried penises haven’t been thoroughly studied, Dr. Montague sees more men in his office today dealing with the issue than he has in the past. His booked-up calendar is no coincidence. The World Health Organization reported that 1 in 7 people is living with obesity. And even though we are living in the golden age of GLP-1s, half of adults worldwide are expected to be overweight or obese by 2050.

The penis itself has two erection chambers. About one-third is inside the body and two-thirds are outside in the penile shaft. When you’re overweight or with severe obesity—and noticing a change in length—what’s changing is basically how much of your penis is inside your body and how much is outside.

“With extreme obesity, it’s like the obese body engulfs the penis. A full buried penis is when the entire penis shaft is buried below the surface of the skin,” says Dr. Montague. So it's still there, but you just can't see it all.

There are a few factors, some starting at birth.

There is a low chance of a baby being born with abnormalities in which the ligaments that attach to the penis are weak. Fortunately, Dr. Dubin says this is usually identified and surgically corrected early on.

Another cause can be “any surgery on your penis” that’s gone wrong, says Dr. Dubin. That includes circumcision, a procedure done on 71.2 percent of American guys are circumcised at birth (or even as an adult). If a doctor does not remove an appropriate amount of foreskin, there is a risk of the penis dropping back into the fatty area surrounding it.

But any procedure that involves the penis runs the risk of scar tissue and possible infection. That includes penile injections and penis enlargement.

“The ironic part is that while a lot of these things try to make your penis look bigger or be bigger, the complication is it can make your penis actually smaller and cause buried penis,” says Dr. Dubin.

Lymphedema can cause swelling in the scrotum. As lymph fluid accumulates, you might see the makings of a buried penis.

Also known as acne inversa, the skin condition can create small and extremely painful lumps under the skin. Lumps usually appear in the armpits, inner thighs, and—you guessed it—your groin. This happens because the body mistakenly attacks hair follicles in the sweat glands, causing pockets of pus and other scarring.

Sometimes when the prostate is removed due to prostate cancer surgery, men may lose about an inch of penile length. “That’s the result of pulling the urethra up to attach to the bladder,” says Dr. Montague. Still, it seems like most penile shortening rebounds as time passes after your surgery, so don't let that stop you from getting your prostate checked.

The condition occurs when scar tissue forms in the penis and it causes a curved erection. The curvature may give the appearance of a shorter length. The issue often arises because of injury to the penis during sex or micro-tears that accumulated from sports or physical accidents affecting the genital area. Fortunately, if diagnosed early, there are drugs and procedures that could straighten out the penis. Read more about it here.

WHEN YOU HAVE a buried penis, certain activities become more difficult. Right away, you might find it hard to pee. Dr. Dubin says guys may not be able to get the stream started and might have poor flow. Buried penis can also cause trouble in the form of dribbling and incontinence.

The area is also tough to clean, which leads to a host of issues that might trigger fungal or bacterial infections or skin breakdown. Buried penis is associated, for example, with a rare skin condition called lichen sclerosus. It’s an inflammatory disorder that causes itchy patches. The skin that hosts these patches becomes very delicate.

Beyond that, penetrative sex remains an ongoing issue. “These men can’t have sex or sex is very difficult,” says Dr. Montague.

Mentally, having a buried penis can cause depression and self-esteem issues. “Most men associate their penis with their masculinity, and they associate fertility with masculinity,” says Dr. Dubin. “These kinds of issues can have strong mental health implications.”

IF YOUR BURIED penis is obesity-related, losing weight will definitely help. But keep in mind that, even after weight loss, you might have excess skin and tissue that keeps your penis buried.

“Sometimes, unfortunately, in very obese people, the damage has already been done, and more aggressive treatment options may be required,” Dr. Dubin. In that scenario, you may need surgery—which is also an option for people who don’t lose weight. Dr. Miller says surgeons would focus on removing the surrounding fat and tissue and repositioning the penis.

“That allows the penis to come outside of the body,” says Dr. Montague. “In most buried penis surgeries, skin and fat are cut out.”

Having a buried penis can be embarrassing to talk about, Dr. Dubin says. It's disturbing to have this happen and it can take a toll on your well-being. If you feel it's getting in the way of living fully, consider talking with a counselor or other mental health professional. It's also crucial to see a doctor if you’re having urinary, hygiene, or sexual health issues because of a buried penis.

She also writes the best-selling Substack newsletter Two Truths and is a co-founder of the non-profit Chamber of Mothers.

#5081. Zcash NU7 upgrade goes live, leaves 12,224 ZEC stranded in Sprout pool

Zcash’s NU7 upgrade has rendered about $15 million in legacy Sprout funds unspendable, although a future network upgrade could potentially restore access.

Zcash’s NU7 network upgrade activated Friday, making about $15 millionin ZEC held in the network’s legacy Sprout shielded pool unspendable.

The upgrade took effect at block 3,543,405 at 02:34 UTC, according to blockchain tracker ZecStats, which lists the Sprout pool balance at about 12,224 ZEC.

The upgrade cuts Zcash’s target time between blocks from 75 seconds to 25, although actual block intervals will adjust toward the new target as mining difficulty changes.

Under Zcash Improvement Proposal (ZIP) 2003, NU7 rejects version 4 transactions, the only format that supports spending Sprout funds. The remaining coins cannot be transferred under the new rules, but have not been burned.

The specification leaves open the possibility that a future network upgrade could restore access, either by re-enabling version 4 transactions or introducing another mechanism to retrieve the funds. However, it warns that “the ability to spend Sprout funds may never be re-enabled.”

NU7 also introduces a Network Sustainability Mechanism that removes 60% of transaction fees from circulation for eventual reissuance through future mining rewards, starting around February 2031.

The upgrade preserves Zcash’s 21 million ZEC supply cap and halving schedule, while introducing limits on shielded activity designed to reduce wallet synchronization demands.
