#5079. US Sanctions 45 More Targets Tied to Alleged Crypto Scam Empire Prince Group

US Sanctions 45 More Targets Tied to Alleged Crypto Scam Empire Prince Group

The U.S. Treasury Department has sanctioned four individuals and 41 entities that it says support Prince Group, the Cambodian conglomerate accused of running scam compounds that use trafficked workers to defraud victims through crypto investment and romance scams.

The new designations, announced on Thursday by the Office of Foreign Assets Control (OFAC), target the offshore companies, nominee owners and administrators that Treasury says have helped Prince Group executives conceal assets and evade earlier sanctions imposed in October 2025 and June 2026.

"Cyber scam syndicates continue to steal billions of dollars from American victims while relying on sophisticated international business networks to launder their illicit proceeds," Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said.

Among those sanctioned is Qiu Yong, who Treasury said controls a network of British Virgin Islands and Hong Kong companies on behalf of Prince Group, including money lenders and wine businesses, and who has worked for a company controlled by Prince Group chairman Chen Zhi. His personal assistant, Zhao Mengyao, was also designated.

OFAC also targeted two people it said are holding property for, or running companies with, Prince Group executive Chen Bo, who was sanctioned in June. They include his wife, He Yujing, to whom Chen Bo transferred ownership of his Tokyo mansion after the October 2025 sanctions, according to Treasury, and associate Visal Pang, who controls 11 Cambodian companies spanning construction, logistics, a hotel and a "super app." Eleven companies still controlled by Chen Bo were also sanctioned, including Prince International Airlines and CB General Insurance.

Further entities in Singapore, Mauritius and Malaysia were sanctioned for links to Prince Group associate Dai An, along with a Singapore media company owned by alleged Prince Group finance chief Thet Li.

Separately, OFAC designated Chui Kok Wa, a Malaysian national based in Madagascar, who Treasury said led a network of scammers that used crypto and other technologies to defraud Americans of more than $400,000.

The U.S. is seeking the forfeiture of about $14 billion in Bitcoin tied to Prince Group, while UK authorities have frozen the London penthouse of Thet Li, Decrypt reported last month. Prince Group has denied the allegations.

Chen Zhi himself was arrested in Cambodia in January along with two other Chinese nationals and extradited to China, where state media described him as the head of a major cross-border gambling and fraud syndicate, the BBC reported. Cambodia, which had revoked his citizenship the month before, also placed the group's Prince Bank into liquidation.

Thursday's action was coordinated with the FBI, the Justice Department's Scam Center Strike Force, which has reported $580 million in crypto seizures and freezes, and the U.S. Attorney's Office for the District of Alaska, with help from authorities in the British Virgin Islands and the UK, according to Treasury.

Interpol declared scam compound networks a global threat last year, citing their use of human trafficking, online fraud and coerced labor. In September, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network linked about $12.7 billion in reported financial activity to suspected crypto investment scams, largely run by criminal organizations operating scam compounds in Southeast Asia.

The UN Office on Drugs and Crime estimated in July that scam operations across East and Southeast Asia, Australia and New Zealand cost victims between $88.3 billion and $114.1 billion in 2025, much of it through crypto investment fraud run from compounds holding people from at least 80 countries.

#5077. Alan Cumming Can’t Be Tamed

At 61, the Traitors host has made a career of playing by his own rules. From leading his dark new series Tip Toe to reprising his X-Men role as Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday, he's always eager to take on a different form.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

“I’M GEARING UP towards hacking it all off,” Alan Cumming says as he twists a finger around an errant silver tendril of hair dangling across his forehead. “I usually like to end a job and do something—get it cut off or get it colored. Something.”

The 61-year-old Tony-winning-Broadway-star-turned-Emmy-winning-reality-TV-host is the consummate chameleon. In just the past year, he’s played the “dandy Scottish laird” host of The Traitors: New Blood, reprised his role as the X-Men’s resident lizard-like teleporting mutant Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday, and took on one of his most emotionally and physically challenging roles as gay bar owner Leo in Tip Toe.

The impulse to change—both on screen and off—is therapeutic. “Over the years, I've had so many different hairstyles, I think, as a reaction to my childhood trauma about my father's obsession with my hair,” he says. Cumming’s memoir, Not My Father’s Son, opens with a scene of his father taking a rusty pair of sheep shears to his temple in a fit of rage. “They were blunt and dirty and they cut my skin, but my father shaved my head with them, holding me down like an animal,” Cumming writes in the first chapter.

The trauma followed him into early adulthood, and he experienced visceral anxiety whenever he got his hair cut. Eventually, however, the act of transformation transformed Cumming. “Hair was always something I felt I had no control over as a child,” he says. “So I wanted to have as much control over it and change it as much as possible, almost in a slightly perverse way.”

Of course, the choice to change isn’t always his, and sometimes the choice is taken away from him. It was “painful,” he says, to be unable to change his hair when he was working on certain projects, like his seven seasons on The Good Wife. “Over the years, I've had so many different hairstyles, but I don't often have my own hair,” Cumming adds. “It's usually for some job.”

Today, however, his natural silver hair seems purposefully unstyled as his fingers coax through it. That is, when he isn’t using his hands to assume the statuesque pose of his Traitors persona or demonstrate the boxing stance from his Marvel superhero training. Simply talking about a performance becomes a performance, his eyes bulging behind a pair of thick-rimmed black glasses and his arms gesturing widely underneath his faded red T-shirt as he describes the immense physicality required of these recent roles.

The walls of his Scottish home are white and surprisingly sparse. You’d have no idea that the person living there is now, by his own admission, best known for “swanning around” a Scottish castle “like an insane little prince” in increasingly outrageous outfits (so outrageous, in fact, that Traitors contestants often applaud when he enters the room after a wardrobe change). Or perhaps the walls are white so he stands out all the more. Either way, the Scottish charm is working overtime on a Sunday evening.

“I think both with my hair and, more lately with what I wear, I do sort of feel like a little dolly that people dress up,” he says before adding, with a good-natured laugh, “I don't mind it.”

In a brief moment between transformations, Cumming sat down with Men’s Health to discuss his ever-changing style on Traitors, how he trained for Avengers: Doomsday, and what he considers to be the ultimate fountain of youth.

MEN’S HEALTH: You often talk about your fashion choices with each Traitors season, but what goes into choosing your hairstyle?

ALAN CUMMING: There was a feeling that because we were doing the civilian version, we could reboot and bring things down a little. So, we decided just to go a little more simple and classic with the hair. But it was hilarious, because every morning I would walk downstairs and say, ‘This is toning it down. What the fuck?’ I guess I wasn't dressed as a coffin or a bride. The plan was to go back to the dandy Scottish laird a little, but it still was pretty mental. But that's why I didn't have lots of color and things in my hair. But we're about to start another Traitors season in a couple of weeks, so all bets are off again.

MH: You're in your 60s now, and you still have wonderful hair. Did you ever have a fear of going bald when you were younger, or would that have maybe been freeing in a way?

AC: I've never feared going bald. I don't think I would care. It's easy when you’re not the bald person saying this, but I don't quite think going bald has the same power it used to. I feel like so many people shave their heads nowadays anyway. And there are loads of actors I know who are bald and wear wigs all the time. When you're an actor, it's obviously easier—and actually probably better—to be bald, because you get more hairdo options.

MH: Outside of Traitors, you're also reprising your role as Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday. How was doing your own stunts in your 60s?

AC: I did some stunt work, but I didn't do all my own stunts. Please don't think that. There are lots of things I'm not allowed to tell you about. I got into trouble for giving the game away earlier on. But I mean, I fight some people. There's loads of fighting in it. If you enjoy fighting and you enjoy superheroes, this movie's for you.

I loved the training. They always train you for much more than you actually need, but I did it. I was boxing with these French boys. And I’d just made a film in France, so they were very impressed with my French and the fact that I could box a little and not have a heart attack.

It’s really funny when people, especially younger people, are so impressed by your fitness. I'm quite bendy as well, so people are always impressed by my flexibility. And I guess I am 61, but, I mean, I'm not dead. I'm not a thousand years old. I've luckily not had any sort of physical things going wrong. I don't really feel any different, to be honest, but it’s also hilarious when young people are just amazed that you've been able to stand up and not die.

MH: How long were you doing this boxing training for?

AC: I only did a week or so with those boys. But I used to take boxing lessons in New York, and I really want to get back into it again. It's such good exercise, especially before you actually get up and spar—all the core stuff and strength stuff—and it's such fun as well.

My boxing teacher was called Mo, and he'd been a big sort of world champion. But one day I went to the gym, and he was really pissed off because he’d seen a music video with some rappers and singers, and they were all playing boxers. He said, "They were just terrible."

And he went, "Drake. Drake was the worst."

And he said to me, "You could take Drake." So now, every time I see Drake, I just think, “I could take you.” Drake better watch out.

MH: Let him know it's on sight. How else did you prepare your body for Avengers?

AC: I actually stay pretty fit all the time. When I'm doing The Traitors, I go to the gym every day, or I do three days gym, two days swim or yoga. There have been times in my life when I've had to get really, really super fit or super skinny or something, so you go nuts. But I like being fit, and I like stretching. I like doing things like that, so I’m pretty constant.

Filming Tip Toe was much more physically demanding, because I knew I was going to have a lot of emotional weight to carry. It was a really disturbing show. So, I would exercise a lot, but I would also get two Thai massages on the weekend because my body was so tense.

If you do a film like X-Men and you're jumping around, yes, sometimes your body can get hurt in that way, but emotionally it wasn't really very demanding, whereas Tip Toe was so intense. Physically, I'm so stressed a lot of the time, and David Morrissey, the other actor, is shoving me around. So, my body was all screwed up. What's really important in those situations is to plan ahead and to make space for you to give your body some chance to recover.

MH: How do you think about your body differently as you take on each new role?

AC: I'm a very physical actor. I think about how the person physically behaves and how they move and stand and… [laughs]. I'm laughing because my husband is just over there, and he always watches me in things and says, "Oh, you look so different. You just disappeared into this character." And then there'll be a scene when I'm running, and he can tell it's me because I run in a weird way. My running always gives me away. But you know who's got the weirdest run ever? Woody Allen. I was once in a film with him and he had to run, and I've never seen anything like it. It just was like, "How is this all happening? How are all these limbs managing to propel someone forward?" It was the craziest thing.

But I'm always very conscious of the physicality of a character. The Traitors is a big example. He's very erect, very sort of grand, quite dancy. He moves like a dancer. And then in Tip Toe, it was really fascinating becoming more cowed as the show went on. He got less confident, so I got more crushed.

With Nightcrawler, he's really fascinating because he's like a little weird lizard or something. So it's a lot of neck stuff. In the first film, I did a lot of work with a movement person to develop the language of that. So, I just remembered all that stuff for Avengers. I suppose it’s muscle memory. After 22 years, I really did remember all that stuff. Also, they gave me a gray wig, which I thought was hilarious. That was a concession to my elder statesman-like thing. I've got gray hair as Nightcrawler now, which is just hilarious. Nothing else has changed, just his hair has grown gray.

MH: You mentioned your longtime husband, Grant. What do you think makes a relationship last?

AC: Having a laugh, being open and kind. Being kind is the biggest thing, but being honest and always trying to talk things through and having a laugh. Also, having time apart.

When I go away to make a film or whatever, we don't see each other for a few weeks. It's actually really great because you miss that person, and you look forward to seeing them and you value the time you do have. It's not just about missing them, but you remember the things that you've always liked, and you're constantly reassessing your relationship because you're looking forward to seeing that person again. Also, it means you maintain your own identity. Obviously, we're a very strong couple, but we have our own lives as well.

So it's a combination of those things, but I really do think you can't underestimate how much being able to laugh is. Also, I think it's good to still have sex. Sex and laughter. I think if either of those go or both of those go, get out, taxi for one. Because they're both the same thing, I suppose—they're sensual pleasures. Those are very important for me, anyway.

MH: Agreed. And so, you mentioned that you're 61 now—

MH: As we all should. But tell me, what's the best part about being 61?

AC: There are lots of nice things. I mean, partly that I forget I'm 61. On the show I'm doing right now, I feel like I can just chat with the kids on the crew who are in their 20s. For me, what's great is being in a position where I'm able to have such strong, long relationships, friendships. With Tip Toe, David Morrissey and I have known each other since the late '80s. Acting against each other in such a horrible way was so great because we were so close and trusted each other. And that was a really good thing about being older—you're so sure of someone and so trust someone. Also, I just love having a life where I do have friends in their 20s and 30s and 40s.

I equate oldness with lack of curiosity. You can be very old and still be very curious, but whenever anyone says, “Oh, I'm too old for this,” I think it's like a death knell somewhere. A little fairy dies when you say that.

I enjoy not giving so much of a fuck. I'm really happy in my life and things don't bother me as much as they used to. I think it'll all come out in the wash. We'll work it out. I feel I'm much more sanguine about things, especially as I've got such a crazy life with so many things going on. It's always this sort of mental schedule and trying to make it all work, but I just now think, “It'll be fine.” So that's a good thing about being older.

MH: What are you most curious about these days?

AC: Lots of things. I'm running a theater now and every day there are things I've got to deal with and talk about and find out about, and that's amazing. I joined TikTok today, so I decided to do that. I want to learn to play the bagpipes. I want to learn to type properly because, I mean, I use my computer, obviously, and I've written books and films, but I've never really properly learned to type. And I want to get better at Spanish, and I have a sort of trad wife fantasy of making gluten-free scones in my AGA, which is a kind of country stove.

AC: I don't really feel this age, but when I was 33, I moved to New York, and I feel like my life hasn't really changed that much since then. I still live in the same area. I still have a lot of the same friends. I do a lot of the same things. I have the same kind of life. I don't go out as much as I used to, but I love dancing still, and I would do that more if people didn't take photos of me all the time. Before mobile phone cameras, you didn't have to worry about being off your tits and dancing in a club with your chums. Now you do.

I'm obviously not 33. I don't look 33. But I feel like I'm as fit as I was when I was 33, kind of.

#5076. Binance’s $6.3B hack leaves questions over hot wallet safeguards

Experts say blockchain records show repeated wallet drains but cannot establish Binance’s internal signing controls or transaction limits.

Binance’s $6.3 billion security incident has left unanswered questions about how the exchange managed its hot wallets and limited the amount attackers could access.

Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said on Oct. 30 that the attack affected the exchange’s own hot wallet. Most customer assets were held in offline cold storage and moved to hot wallets only when users requested withdrawals.

Wallet balances offer only a partial picture of an exchange’s exposure to an attack. Public blockchain records can show funds moving but cannot establish the internal controls governing those transfers, CertiK analyst Jonathan Riss told Cointelegraph.

In its initial report Tuesday, Binance said attackers accessed an internal wallet-signing service to move funds outside its normal withdrawal process. Binance said some technical details would remain confidential to prevent further attacks.

The exchange did not respond to Cointelegraph’s questions about its wallet balances and safeguards.

Rivals including Coinbase, Gate and Upbit have disclosed their cold-storage ratios following the attack.

Riss said the $6.3 billion figure represented the cumulative value of assets affected across 10 blockchains. On Ethereum, attackers repeatedly drained a hot wallet as new deposits arrived.

When attackers can obtain valid signatures, their potential access depends on what the signing system allows them to transfer over time, he said. A wallet’s balance at a single moment does not capture that exposure.

Related: EIP-7702 didn’t cause Binance hack, but may have amplified losses

Transaction limits enforced by the application requesting a signature can be bypassed if that application is compromised, Riss said. Limits enforced independently by the signing system may still restrict transfers.

“A comparison with other exchanges isn’t really possible from the outside,” Riss added. “Public information on internal signing architecture is limited everywhere, and reserve distribution and proof-of-reserves attestations show what sits where, not how a withdrawal is authorized.”

BitGo chief product officer Eugene Hahr also declined to speculate about Binance’s internal safeguards.

“The breakdown of custody arrangements is a question Binance is best placed to answer,” he said.

Idan Ofrat, co-founder and chief product officer at Fireblocks, said exchanges typically keep enough funds in hot wallets to cover a few days of expected withdrawals, often representing a low single-digit percentage of assets under management.

Exchanges generally hold the bulk of customer assets in cold storage, while some use intermediate wallets that require manual approval for transfers to hot wallets, he said.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

Alex Tobol, chief technology officer at Telegram-based crypto wallet Walt, said holding billions of dollars in hot wallets is not inherently unusual for an exchange of Binance’s size.

Hot wallets generally process withdrawals automatically, Tobol said, but replenishing them from cold storage should require a separate treasury decision and manual approval.

Hahr said controls applied before signing, including transaction limits, approved withdrawal addresses and additional authorization requirements, can help contain losses when a system is compromised.

Magazine: Binance hackers raced to move billions before it could be frozen

#5074. Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds lost $430.2 million to net withdrawals Thursday as a technology-stock selloff spread to crypto, with Fidelity and BlackRock leading the exits.

Fidelity's Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) posted $199.9 million in net outflows and BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) shed $105 million, according to Farside Investors. Together they accounted for about 71% of the day's withdrawals.

Grayscale's GBTC lost another $49.9 million, while Franklin Templeton's EZBC shed $32.9 million. No fund in Farside's table recorded positive net flows Thursday, although several reported no net movement.

The latest withdrawals brought this week's net outflow to $869.8 million, following a $643.2 million outflow last Friday. Wednesday's modest $74.6 million inflow offered a brief reprieve before Thursday's reversal.

Fidelity's swing was particularly sharp: FBTC attracted $236.7 million Wednesday, then surrendered nearly $200 million the next session. BlackRock, meanwhile, lost $201.2 million even on Wednesday's positive day, extending a five-session withdrawal streak since October 30 to roughly $1.04 billion.

ETF flows have often been treated as a gauge of demand from investors accessing Bitcoin through regular brokerage accounts. The latest figures show that access works both ways: the same familiar wrapper that makes buying easy also makes exiting straightforward.

Because ETFs trade on stock exchanges, investors can adjust their Bitcoin exposure without transferring coins to a crypto wallet. That convenience can also make the funds an easy place to cut risk during an equity selloff. Daily net flows capture shares created or redeemed at the fund level, not every trade between investors.

Thursday's redemptions arrived as the Nasdaq Composite plunged 5.5% and the S&P 500 fell about 4.4%. Chipmakers and data-center stocks led the decline as investors questioned the pace of artificial-intelligence spending and potential restrictions after the U.S. midterm elections.

Bitcoin also surrendered its post-election rebound, falling back toward $76,000 after trading above $80,000 Wednesday. Short liquidations had helped power that rally, making the subsequent reversal more painful for leveraged traders.

The ETF figures add another piece to that picture. Net flows measure creations minus redemptions of ETF shares; they cannot tell whether holders were raising cash to cover losses elsewhere, trimming Bitcoin exposure, or rebalancing portfolios.

#5073. The Man Behind Fashion’s Most Dazzling Spectacles Turns His Attention to the Art of Light

For as long as he can remember, Alexandre de Betak has been entranced by light. As a 10-year-old, he rewired his Paris bedroom so that visitors had to be buzzed in, greeted by shifting colored lights. He went on to found Bureau Betak, a set design agency that transformed the runway with its megawatt spectacles for clients like Dior, Gucci, Jacquemus, and Fendi. De Betak estimates that he produced “thousands” of fashion shows—including Saint Laurent’s spring-summer 2020 ready-to-wear extravaganza, for which he bathed the Eiffel Tower in golden light, and installations for Rodarte featuring fluorescent and neon tubes.

A new truss light sculpture by Alexandre de Betak, seen here in a 17th-century Swedish castle

Now, de Betak, who sold his company in 2021, is turning the spotlight on his own creative practice. On November 5, he opened his first solo exhibition of artworks at Salon 94 in New York. The electronically programmed Truss Light Sculptures are inspired by the scaffolding and fixtures of his early runway shows and in some cases literally made from them. “That equipment is old-fashioned now, but I wanted to honor it,” de Betak says. “And I liked the irony that I spent so much energy over the years trying to hide it.”

On view at Salon 94’s Upper East Side town house through December 19, the exhibit presents two series. In the gallery’s contemporary space, the first is composed of freestanding and wall-mounted aluminum structures fitted with LED bulbs. The upstairs Stone Room, a historic space with Caen stone plaster walls and arched windows, is the backdrop for a second series of versions in bronze. To make them, de Betak had a French foundry recast each part down to the smallest screw by hand, a process that sometimes required more than 20 molds.

A pair of wall-mounted sculptures in aluminum

The light sculptures, which vary from sconce-like creations to hulking constructions out of a sci-fi movie, have a sly sense of humor and a human quality. This is especially true when they come to life, thanks to a remote-control-operated system that activates light sequences that glow, sparkle, thrum, or react to ambient noise, “whether a whisper,” de Betak says, “or extremely loud music.” For these effects, he drew on his stage work, where he was renowned for cueing, or choreographing, light and sound. “I’m interested in the ephemeral nature of what this equipment was made to show—whether a fashion show or a ballet,” he says. “At a moment when everything is virtual, I think it’s more important than ever to create things that produce a live emotion.”

Salon 94’s founder Jeanne Greenberg Rohatyn observes that de Betak’s sculptures are equally powerful switched on or off. Unlit they remind her of David Smith artworks or Russian constructivism. Lit, and with synced sound, they evoke the theatricality of stage performers. “Alex has spent his career building structures that disappear into someone else’s spectacle,” she says. “Here, the structure is the whole point.”

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

From smart swim goggles to self-cleaning vacuums.

DO YOU REALLY need an app for everything? Men’s Health’s Reviews Team spent the year trying gadgets that promise to make life easier, and often come with another login or an AI feature to figure out first. Resident tech guys—including Senior Gear Editor Ryan Brower, Fitness Editor Charles Thorp, and Senior Editor Brett Williams—have tried enough to know which ones are worth the trouble and your hard-earned dollars.

Their favorites make up this year’s Tech Awards, from swim goggles that show your pace in the lens to a massage gun that runs for up to four hours per charge and still fits in your gym bag. We also found time-saving gadgets like a robot vacuum that washes its own mop, so you can cross one more chore off your weekend list.

#5070. GOPAX halts crypto trading, withdrawals to move all assets to cold storage

South Korea’s GOPAX suspended trading, deposits and standard withdrawals after a hacking threat, with manual withdrawals available through support.

South Korean crypto exchange GOPAX suspended trading, deposits and standard withdrawals Friday after receiving an email threatening a hack, according to local newspaper Kyunghyang Shinmun.

A GOPAX representative told the newspaper that no hack or attempted theft had occurred at that point.

GOPAX said it halted deposits and standard withdrawals at 5:55 am UTC. Trading stopped at 6:53 UTC, according to a second announcement. All unfilled orders were canceled.

GOPAX said it plans to transfer all crypto assets to offline wallets to protect users following a series of leaks and hacking incidents affecting financial institutions. Its notices cited financial regulators’ recommendations.

Cointelegraph reached out to GOPAX for comment but had not received a response by publication time.

Customers can still request withdrawals through GOPAX’s support center, but the exchange warned that manual verification and approvals could delay processing.

The suspension came days after South Korea’s Financial Services Commission (FSC) urged domestic exchanges to increase cold-wallet holdings following the $6.3 billion Binance hack. South Korean exchanges must keep at least 80% of customer crypto assets in cold storage.

Financial Supervisory Service data previously showed that GOPAX held 80.54% of user assets offline at the end of September, near the required minimum.

In October, the FSC reported cybersecurity deficiencies at 130 of 555 financial companies reviewed nationwide. The regulator also identified GOPAX operator Streami and crypto exchange Prabang among 16 companies involved in earlier security incidents, prompting plans for tighter cybersecurity oversight.

South Korean exchange Prabang also suspended services after detecting abnormal withdrawals from its hot wallet on Oct. 24. The exchange reported about 100 million won ($70,000) in losses, according to Yonhap News Agency. Prabang said it would reimburse affected customers using company funds.

Related: South Korea seeks 4th crypto tax delay, targets 2029 start

#5069. How to Organize a Kitchen Pantry in 6 Easy Steps, According to Experts

A pantry is among the most hardworking areas of the home, but without proper skills, it can also turn into the most cluttered. Learning how to organize a kitchen pantry like a pro requires a pro, so we tapped expert organizers to share their best pantry organization tips. Ahead, you’ll find a step-by-step guide to organizing your pantry—and keeping it that way. The process includes decluttering expired items, grouping pantry staples by category, deciding what to place on upper and lower shelves, and utilizing food storage containers and labeled bins. Whether you have a spacious walk-in pantry or you’re working with limited shelf space, use these pantry organization ideas to maximize vertical space, function, and style.

Start by removing everything from your pantry. “As you are taking the items out, take note of expiration dates and toss anything that is past its best by date, that is stale, or is no longer edible,” says Megan Barnhart, a professional organizer and founder of Barnhart Home. Since the pantry is rarely empty, take the opportunity to deep-clean it—wipe pull-out shelves, wash pantry storage vessels, and sweep the floor. You can also use this time to place liners or contact paper on the shelves. “Liners prevent damage to the wood and paint and make future clean-ups so much easier,” says Catherine Benson, a cleaning and organization aficionado based in Lexington, Kentucky.

Removing everything from the pantry will help you survey what you have in order to organize it better.

Before you return everything into the pantry, group items by category. There are several ways to do this, and experts recommend choosing the method that best suits you. One idea is to group like items together. “Think of your pantry as your own personal grocery store—canned goods together, pastas together, baking ingredients together,” Barnhart shares.

Another option is to categorize items by how you shop and plan meals. “If you shop with specific meals in mind and like to meal plan, group those ingredients together; for example, create a dinner zone that includes individual categories or bins such as pasta, grains, and soup,” says Lisa Eckerle, a professional organizer and owner of The Designer Organizer. If you shop by stocking up on pantry goods, categories like canned goods, baking supplies, and condiments may make more sense. “We normally use a hybrid approach for most of our clients, creating zones based on activity and categories within the zones based on food type,” Eckerle shares.

Once you’ve organized your categories, you might want containers to hold them. But before purchasing any storage vessels, measure pantry shelves, noting their width, depth, and height. Barnhart and Eckerle recommend incorporating the following storage solutions to maximize space and add style.

Risers are one of Barnhart’s favorite ways to store canned goods. “It makes it much easier to see the canned items in the back as well as what is in the front, and I feel it also helps avoid over-buying or repurchasing because you can see what you actually have,” she says.

Turntables are a must-have for pro organizers. “With all L-shaped pantries, we always start by placing turntables in most of the corners; these help maximize that space, avoid food getting lost or spoiled in the back, and create a cohesive visual,” Eckerle says. She notes they also work well on pantry shelves above eye level.

Consider containers and other tools to help store items in your pantry more effectively.

Turntables are great for oils and vinegars, sauces, condiments, broths, and spices. “If you have items you use daily, like nut butters, honey, and spreads that do not require refrigeration, consider putting those items on their own lazy Susan as well; it makes it easy to grab what you need and also makes it easy to clean up small spills and drips because they are contained to the lazy Susan,” Barnhart says.

Barnhart suggests using beautiful baskets or clear containers to group together things like snacks and dry goods. “This contains the items so they are not spilling out onto the shelves or floor and it is also much more aesthetically appealing as well,” Barnhart says.

One of Eckerle’s favorite recent hacks are wire cooling racks on the interior bottom of wooden storage bins. “By adding a plastic shelf liner under the cooling rack for easy cleaning, we created a unique fruit bin that was able to match the rest of the pantry’s decor and still store the fruit correctly with proper airflow,” she shares.

Now it’s time to add everything back into the pantry. “The most-reached-for categories should be near the door or center, with back stock and lesser-used category items placed in the back or the sides,” Eckerle says. She suggests putting meal-related items in the most accessible location, especially breakfast items, so they’re easy to grab when you’re in a rush.

Avoid storing seldom-used or heavy, small appliances on the top shelf or floor. “To prevent injury, damage, and frustration, those items should be stored between knee and shoulder height,” Eckerle says. Additionally, consider at what height you want each category. For example, “whether you want items out of reach of young children or dogs, or accessible and at eye level for children,” Eckerle says.

Keep things you grab often towards the front, while less-used items can go in the back.

“I am a big fan of labeling in the pantry—it helps systems stay in place when everyone knows what goes where and where to put it back,” Barnhart says. A label maker works great for this, but even stickers and sharpies will do.

Once you know how to organize a kitchen pantry, it’s important to maintain it. Eckerle recommends lightly resetting the pantry after every grocery haul. Additionally, she advises scheduling a day each quarter for a thorough pantry reset. “Re-check expiration dates, dispose of open food, and throw away or donate the items/categories that are no longer on your menu,” she says. You can also use this time to consider any seasonal menu changes that may impact how you organize your pantry. “Depending on the season, you may purchase different ingredients or cook different types of meals,” says Shweta Thota, a lifestyle content creator and Intel hardware engineer based in Sacramento. “This may require rearranging your pantry to accommodate seasonal items.” For example, in the winter, you may want to have a setup that works for more canned goods.

#5068. Greece approves 10% tax on crypto gains starting in 2027

Greece has approved a 10% tax on cryptocurrency gains starting in 2027, with an exemption for investors whose annual net gains do not exceed 500 euros.

Greece’s parliament approved legislation imposing a 10% tax on cryptocurrency gains, establishing new rules for investors as the country prepares to introduce the levy in 2027.

The Hellenic Parliament passed the tax measures Thursday as part of a broader bill covering loan oversight, debt protections and financial market regulation.

The bill introduces a 10% tax on profits from crypto asset sales but exempts investors whose annual gains do not exceed 500 euros ($560), local financial publication Oikonomikos Tachydromos reported Friday. The new framework is scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027.

The legislation also imposes a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and providing liquidity to decentralized protocols. Using crypto to buy goods or services may also trigger capital gains tax, while swapping one crypto asset for another does not generate taxable gains, the report notes.

Greece’s Ministry of National Economy and Finance first proposed the tax in October. The legislation gives investors 12 months to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto gains. They must pay the resulting tax within 60 days of filing to avoid penalties and interest.

#5067. Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Thousands of Paraguayans marched through Asunción this week demanding their money back after Ueno Bank, a digital lender, locked about 3 million customers out of their accounts, according.

That is about 60% of Paraguay’s adults. The bank also held $800 million to $850 million in public money, including state pension and health funds, according to the AP, so the fallout reaches the pension system.

Anger boiled over Thursday night outside Ueno’s headquarters in Asunción, where savers burned debit and credit cards. Paraguayo “Payo” Cubas, a former presidential candidate, tried to join and was booed, shoved, and forced out.

Protesters shouted “divisor” and “oportunista,” meaning divider and opportunist, accusing him of trying to profit politically from public rage, according to the same report.

Diario Hoy reported that protesters also blamed him for the Senate’s current makeup, saying Cubas put Zenaida Delgado and Javier Vera on his party’s legislative ticket, the slate of candidates voters choose from, and that both joined the government’s side soon after taking office.

#Nacionales | 😱 Echan a "Payo" Cubas de la manifestación

🔹 Paraguayo “Payo” Cubas fue rechazado por los manifestantes cuando intentó acercarse a la protesta. Los presentes lo increparon y le gritaron “divisor” y “oportunista”.

➡️ Además, cuestionaron su responsabilidad en el… pic.twitter.com/odHzVvGPO3

— Universo 970 AM (@Universo970py) November 6, 2026

President Santiago Peña, a shareholder in Ueno’s holding company until March 2025, submitted his resignation Thursday, but a joint session of Congress called to accept it adjourned without a quorum, according to La Nación. He then said he would face impeachment—a trial in Congress to remove a president—and defend himself.

The lower house had already voted 78-0 to open impeachment, and on Friday the Senate rejected his request for 72 extra hours to prepare his defense. The Senate planned a removal vote for Friday night, ABC Color reported, with Vice President Pedro Alliana next in line.

Horacio Cartes, the Colorado Party leader and Peña’s former political mentor, withdrew his support and backed the impeachment. “We will not be on the side of the plunder of public, private and retirees’ funds,” Cartes said.

Interior Minister Enrique Riera and central bank president Carlos Carvallo have resigned, and eight Ueno executives have been arrested, according to La Nación’s Friday update. Prosecutors also opened a criminal investigation into Peña’s possible responsibility for the bank’s irregularities, which he denies.

Police used water cannons and tear gas on marchers near Congress, and a new law requires most deposits to be returned within five business days, though protesters have not been placated.

The central bank says its deposit guarantee covers up to 228.3 million guaraníes per person, about $36,000, but as Decrypt previously reported, the first payouts will go only to accounts with up to 10 million guaraníes (around $1,600) and no debts to Ueno.

Bitcoiners, of course, rushed to use Paraguay’s banking crisis as an example on the benefits of decentralization and self custody, and why the “Bitcoin fixes this” motto is still relevant in today’s financial system. “Bitcoin adoption at a nation-state level can mitigate these types of situations,” said JAN3 founder Samson Mow. “If BTC is available to the people as a parallel rail, then systemic banking failures have far less impact.”

Bitcoin adoption at a nation-state level can mitigate these types of situations. If BTC is available to the people as a parallel rail, then systemic banking failures have far less impact.

“Bitcoin matters now more than ever. Look at Paraguay today,” wrote Stani Kulechov, founder of the DeFi protocol Aave.

The Federation of Educators of Paraguay has called a national strike and march for Monday, Nov. 9, Diario Hoy reported.

#5066. How to Use the Deficit Deadlift to Produce More Power on Your Pulls

Increasing your deadlift's range of motion can help you build strength through the toughest part of the lift.

LITTLE CHANGES CAN yield big results. If you’re working to set new strength standards and push through weight room plateaus, movements like the deficit deadlift stand as testament to that sentiment. The exercise is identical to the traditional barbell deadlift, with one key distinction: You stand on a slightly elevated surface—we're talking just a couple of inches—as you pull the weight off the floor.

There's a good chance you haven't tried this variation before. It's more popular among strength athletes, like powerlifters and strongmen, who toil over every minute detail of their deadlift technique to gain a competitive edge. But just because you haven't done it doesn't mean you shouldn't. Whether you're looking to shake up your routine or address a specific weakness in your deadlift, this simple adjustment is worth trying. Here's what you need to know.

DEFICIT DEADLIFTS EXTEND the range of motion of the pull by the height of whatever you're standing on, usually an inch or two, making the lift more demanding. Athletes who train for strength sports typically add deficit deadlifts to their programs when they're having trouble pulling heavier weights off the floor.

Say you can deadlift 315 pounds, but can't budge 335. You might be struggling to produce enough force during the initial pull. By standing on an elevated surface, you're forcing the muscles responsible for getting the bar moving—primarily your glutes and quads with a little help from your hamstrings and lower back—to work through a greater range of motion.

The extra demand on these muscles “can potentiate more muscle growth and greater strength—especially grip strength,” says Lee Boyce, a Toronto-based personal trainer, college professor, and owner of Boyce Training Systems. “Reaching down further than normal to pick up a bar also means more quadriceps activity, due to the greater knee bend that's required to do so. Transitioning back to a standard deadlift after a phase of deficit work can give the normal stuff the chance to feel ‘easy’ by comparison.”

This concept isn’t simply theoretical. A small study of 20 experienced lifters compared conventional deadlifts, deficit deadlifts, and rack pulls (which have you lifting a heavier weight than you can deadlift over a smaller range of motion). Researchers found more quad and glute activation during deficit deadlifts. The study measured only muscle engagement, not whether that engagement leads to greater muscle growth. Still, what’s clear is this: deficit deadlifts offer another way to challenge your lower body without introducing an entirely different movement.

Boyce says deficit deadlifts can also be a good fit for shorter lifters who want a longer pulling range. “Being shorter means the bar will ‘start’ higher up the leg, making for a shorter range of motion and a pull that may not be as fulfilling,” he explains. “Standing on a platform or a couple of plates can fix this problem.”

That won’t work for everyone, though, especially beginners. Celebrity trainer Don Saladino likes deficit deadlifts but says the added mobility demands mean they're not a good fit for every lifter. “I work with plenty of people who lack the mobility or control to deadlift properly from the floor, and so why would I increase their range of motion?” he shared in an October 20 Instagram post. “In many cases, I’m doing the opposite. I’m elevating the bar to help them achieve a stronger, more controlled starting position.”

Deficit deadlifts demand more mobility in your hips and ankles since you're reaching farther down to grab the bar. If you're excessively rounding your lower back to get into the starting position or your hips shoot up before the bar leaves the floor, you need to address those form issues before diving into deficit deadlifts. Try lifting from a smaller deficit or skip the exercise altogether. Dedicated hip mobility work may help you get there, but deficit deadlifts aren't a must-do exercise. There are plenty of deadlift variations to choose from, and the best one is the one that suits your abilities and training goals.

THE DEFICIT DEADLIFT is more of a modification than a distinct exercise. The instructions below focus on the barbell version, but you can perform just about any type of deadlift from a deficit, including dumbbell deadlifts, kettlebell deadlifts, and trap bar deadlifts. Check out the clip below, then follow the exercise cues.

Step 1: First, establish an elevated surface to stand on. Some strength gyms have dedicated platforms for deficit deadlifts. Make sure the surface is stable and large enough to accommodate your stance.

Step 2: Stand on the elevated surface in front of a loaded barbell, positioning the bar over the middle of your feet. Grab the bar with an overhand grip, just outside your legs. Then, bend your knees until they're next to your arms—not jutting past them or leaving a gap between your knees and arms when viewed from the side. This should naturally put your hips in a good starting position.

Step 3: Take a deep breath into your abdomen and brace your core as if you're preparing to take a punch. Keeping your arms extended, lift your chest as though you're showing off the logo on your shirt. Then, drive through your feet to pull the barbell off the floor.

Step 4: Keep the barbell as close to your legs as possible as you pull it up your shins and thighs. The bar may lightly brush against your legs, but that's okay. Keeping the weight close to your body reduces the demand on your lower back and puts you in a more efficient pulling position.

Step 5: Once you're standing tall with your hips and knees fully extended, reverse the movement by pushing your hips back and bending your knees to lower the barbell to the floor under control.

THE DEFICIT DEADLIFT is still a deadlift, so your pulling techniques shouldn’t change much. Those extra inches, though, will alter your form a bit, so it’s crucial to understand the potential pitfalls you may encounter when trying this subtly different deadlift variation.

An extra inch or two of range doesn't seem like much, but it changes the mechanics of your deadlift and places greater demands on your lower body muscles. Trying to lift the same weights you use for traditional deadlifts may compromise your form, especially if you're not accustomed to the increased range of motion. Start lighter and work your way up.

The first time you program deficit deadlifts, think of starting with 75-80% of your standard working weight. So if you do three hard sets of five reps with 275 pounds for standard deadlifts, you’d start around 205 or 225 pounds for your first deficit deadlift session. Your glutes and hammies will still get plenty of stimulus, and the weight will be light enough for you to handle through the extended range of motion.

It's common to see folks tap the weight on the floor and bounce it into their next deadlift rep (called a touch-and-go rep). At the start of your deficit deadlift programming, don’t do this. Remember that you’re doing deficit deadlifts to strengthen your initial pull from the floor, so you'll get more out of the exercise by treating every rep as a single rep.

After you lower the barbell to the ground, reset your hips, re-tighten your core, and pull the weight as if you’re trying for a one-rep max. You can eventually train deficit deadlifts with touch-and-go mechanics. But first, master the move as a true deadlift.

If you're new to deficit deadlifts, start with a shorter elevation of around an inch. Stick with that height for a few weeks to see how it affects your form and how comfortable you feel pulling from a deeper position. If you can maintain your usual deadlift mechanics, you can experiment with adding another half inch to an inch.

Some powerlifters pull from deficits of two to four inches, sometimes even higher. You don't need to go to those extremes. (“I wouldn’t recommend exceeding six inches,” Boyce adds.) For most lifters, one to two inches is plenty to make the movement more challenging. Instead of continually increasing the height, focus on progressively overloading your deficit deadlift by gradually adding more reps or weight as you get stronger.

YOU CAN USE the deficit deadlift either as a primary strength exercise or as an accessory to your regularly scheduled deadlift regimen. Because they’re more demanding, beginners should start with two to three sets of three to five reps once per week. You can do them after your main deadlift work, if you don’t feel too fatigued, or on a different training day.

“Deficit work is best done in sets of under six reps due to the neural demands of pulling heavy weight beyond the regular range of motion,” Boyce says. “Treating them as a typical strength training exercise would be the smart call here.”

If you’re a more experienced deadlifter, you can sub them in as your primary pulling exercise for a training block (typically four to six weeks). Some competitive powerlifters even work up to a heavy set of one to three reps—but unless you’re training for maximal strength, you don’t need to lift this heavy for deficit deadlifts. Start lighter, get comfortable with the range of motion, and gradually add weight over time.

Outside of writing, Andrew trains in Brazilian Jiu-Jitsu, helps coach his gym's kickboxing team, and enjoys reading and cooking.

#5065. Bitcoin holds $75K as AI regulation fears see KOSPI halted after 8% crash

Bitcoin consolidated as fears over US AI regulation spread to Asia, causing South Korea to announce measures to calm stock-market volatility.

Bitcoin (BTC) held above $75,000 on Friday as a sell-off in US technology stocks spread to Asian markets.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD trading near its 50-week simple moving average (SMA), at $75,733.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 50-week SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The pair fell as much as 6.6% on Thursday as US stock markets saw sustained selling throughout the Wall Street trading session. The S&P 500 and tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite closed down 4.4% and 5.5%, respectively.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

On Friday, Asian stocks extended the sell-off. South Korea’s KOSPI Composite Index fell more than 8% shortly after the open, triggering a circuit breaker that halted trading for 20 minutes. The index then pared losses to close 6% lower at 5,059.69 points, its lowest close since March 31.

KOSPI one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

At an emergency meeting, Korea’s deputy prime minister and finance minister Lee Hyoung-il announced measures to reduce forced selling.

Brokerages will be exempt from a requirement to maintain a 140% collateral ratio on margin loans for three months from Monday, local media reported. Authorities said they would prepare futher measures if volatility increased.

“We recognize with gravity the expanding volatility in global stock markets following the U.S. midterm elections,” Chosun quoted an unnamed government source as saying.

Analysts linked Thursday’s Wall Street losses to fears of tighter AI regulation after Democrats won the House and Senate. Those fears compounded existing market concerns about the sector’s capital spending.

Related: Bitcoin, Ether ETFs post $530M outflows as election gains evaporate

Citing an X post by Democratic Rep. Yassamin Ansari, who called for a “pause on advanced AI development,” market commentary service The Kobeissi Letter said an outright US ban on AI was unlikely.

“In our view, a ban of AI will not happen in the US (with near-certainty). Such a ban, if even possible to enforce, would mean the US surrendering its position in the global AI race to China. That won’t happen,” it wrote in an X post.

Kobeissi noted that Thursday’s losses were concentrated in tech, with the S&P 500 declining just about 2.5% excluding the sector. The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index, which tracks 30 semiconductor companies, finished Thursday 12.2% lower.

Philadelphia Semiconductor Index one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Economist Mohamed El-Erian also called for common ground on AI policyt.said that lawmakers would need to find common ground on AI policy.

“America’s ‘let it rip’ approach to AI aligns with the technology’s productivity potential and national security imperatives. However, it has fallen short not only on safety and diffusion, but also on bringing society along,” he wrote in a Substack note.

El-Erian said the backlash reflected “genuine concerns,” information failures and behavioral factors.

Cointelegraph previously reported that Democrat midterm sweeps under Republican presidents have historically been followed by strong stock returns. Bitcoin’s correlation with the S&P 500, has become more positive in recent months.

#5064. 18 Best Leather Sofas of 2026: Couches That Are Editor-Approved

The best leather sofas adhere to the ABCs of great seating: agnostic design, beautiful texture, and classic silhouettes. Generally, genuine leather is lauded for its durability and longevity—it’s only going to look better with age and some patina. The fabric also makes a wildly comfy, easy-to-clean surface where dirt and dust can be wiped away with a cloth.

If you’re on the search for a new sofa that radiates sophistication (and not just single energy), there are a bounty of leather options at every price point, whether you prefer the softness of suede or the supple, lived-in feel of top-grain leather. Ahead, we gathered our favorite leather couches available online, from small-space designs to larger sectionals that will become the anchor for your future hangouts.

We also included a few wish-list-worthy collectible pieces from midcentury-modern makers like Tobia Scarpa and de Sede, in case contemporary silhouettes don’t quite measure up to the look you’re going for. Below, some of the most handsome leather styles on our wishlist.

Castlery Jonathan top-grain leather sofa is overstuffed, inviting, and supportive—the holy trinity of comfort. We love the inviting modular design that’s perfect for kicking your feet up on the nearby coffee table or simply lounging around in your modern living space. Rachel Fletcher, AD commerce director, has had the Castlery’s Jonathan sectional since 2022 and still gives it an A. The sofa is streamlined—it’s legless look resembles a seat you might find at one of those dine-in theaters. It’s overstuffed and inviting, but still supportive. Contributor Sarah Madaus said it was so comfortable, she asked her partner to cook dinner so she could keep sitting in it. Choose from caramel or warm taupe colorways—or maybe get both, one for the living room and one for the den.

You won’t regret investing in the Article Cigar leather sofa, which has the “soft buttery” rawhide tan texture of a baseball glove, but the rounded curves of a marshmallow. “The Cigar sofa’s back is on the lower side, but I cannot emphasize enough how perfect it is for a New York City apartment with a doorway so narrow it is functionally a nightclub bouncer for furniture,” says digital features editor Charlotte Collins. She noted in her review that the substantial seat padding doesn’t leave any indents and that the silhouette is deep enough to comfortably enjoy a nap. She compares it to “an heirloom piece repurposed for modern interiors.”

The seat on this DWR sofa is leveled up by aluminum legs, so it doesn’t look bulky in the way other grounded designs do. We appreciate the modern details such as the minimal lines and cubic silhouette that inspired Torbjørn Anderssen and Espen Voll, the Nordic designers who created the Outline sofa exclusively for Muuto. It comes in as many as 25 different leather variations that include neutrals like the more traditional cognac to the more design-forward mink and birch. For a smaller footprint, opt for the 67-inch-wide loveseat.

Slim, stilted iron legs give this sofa some midcentury flair—a sound choice of silhouette if you want something trend-resistant that can adapt from one home to the next. Refined details like double-stitched seams and aniline-dyed leather give it staying power. And while a lower back makes it less appealing as a movie night staple, it’s deep enough for occasional snoozing, or serving your reading sessions very well.

Low-slung and clean-lined with a platform base, the Bauhaus-influenced Henley sofa would fit well in a stylish penthouse or add flair to a modern living room flush with chrome accents. The mixed-material sofa’s supple leather softens its industrial-looking stainless-steel foundation. Its tufted backrest brings a sophistication to the design, and slimmer armrests add polish to the overall silhouette.

Not only do AD editors love Quince bedding, but we also have a sweet spot for this Italian leather couch. The low-profile design is classic but not boring, and able to evolve with your design tastes. With two comfy chaises, the design is especially well-suited for spaces where lounging or a conversation pit–style seating is the main goal. Atop the sturdy wooden frame sit layers of plush, high-density foam and removable cushions. Solid wood block legs just peeking out from below complete the look.

This reclining sofa from Crate & Barrel’s Drift line has won over many satisfied reviewers who appreciate that the deep-seated model has a contemporary look and a generous amount of cushioning. Design-wise, the Drift Power Triple is clean-lined, with wide arms, waterfall curves, and camouflaged recliner mechanisms that keep the overall appearance streamlined. The couch’s bold proportions make it a worthy statement piece in your living room or screening space, with especially inviting elements for Netflix binge-a-thons.

Some pieces of furniture just look like they’ll age well, and such is the case with this tufted design from CB2’s Gianfranco Frattini collaboration. An influential architect and designer who helped shape the 1950s and 1960s Italian design movement, Frattini’s ethos is apparent in this sofa, which reimagines traditional elements like tufting for modern tastes. In an unexpected hue like grey, the sofa has the potential to bring richly layered Italian design to any living room.

This classic couch is available in forty different genuine and faux leather options if you’ve been having trouble finding the perfect shade of olive green or a chestnut without red undertones. If that’s too much decision fatigue, we recommend going with a versatile shade of black, mostly because the slim arms bear a resemblance to the luxuriously lived-in black leather sofa we spotted in Walton Goggins’ upstate New York retreat.

Cushy doesn’t even begin to describe the Grantham sofa, whose substantial silhouette can easily take on the load of grounding your entire living room’s design scheme. If lounging is your main goal, extra padding on the already oversized arms eliminates the need for spare throw pillows, while the equally cushioned backrest makes it inviting for future movie nights.

The silhouette of this piece, a collaboration between furniture brand Four Hands and Los Angeles–based interior designer Amber Lewis, feels like an updated take on your parents’ bulky Chesterfield sofa. The built-in cushioning helps give the sofa a more elongated look and, while it could feel too formal for casual spaces, the front legs are fitted with tiny, charming brass casters that bring some unexpected levity to the silhouette.

While a lot of leather sofas look like clones of one another, this three-seater from Soho Home stands out among the pack thanks to its handsome wood frame and casually elevated feel. A shade like chestnut or sangria blend in more seamlessly with the oak wood for a more understated look, while the moss, honey, or classic black hues accentuate the soft edge frame to create a statement piece for your living room.

Maiden Home’s modular Jones Sofa has versatility on its side. Not only is it available in various leather styles and a range of hues, but its pieces are detachable so you can rearrange them to align with your seating needs. Not feeling a three-seater anymore? Convert the set into a loveseat and an armless chair. The low slung sofa draws its design from 1970s influences, with oversized cushions and plush arms. Tailored flange seams add structure to the seams, achieving sophistication without lacking comfort.

Personality is one thing that this vintage de Sede leather couch has no shortage of. Just looking at it makes us want to drape ourselves over the U-shaped frame. Manufactured in Switzerland, the style is all about capturing mid-20th-century craftsmanship that lends itself to comfort. Retro vibes are not to be overlooked.

In a sea of black, brown, and tan leather sofas, Sixpenny’s curved Camino provides you with an opportunity to branch out with its playful “North Atlantic” shade of blue. At the same time, the durable, top-hide nubuck leather is well-suited to handle long naps and family movie nights.

For one, leather couches are durable as hell. Leather is said to last four times longer than a fabric sofa, not to mention the patina of aged leather is currently in vogue. It’s worth it: Durable leather furniture is going to stick with you for the long run. Plus, leather is hypoallergenic. Whatever type of leather you choose, no open fibers means no catching and holding dust or other allergens. (Take that, pet hair!)

If properly cared for, your leather sofa can last a good 15 to 25 years (or even longer)—just look at all the high-quality de Sede designs, Bellini configurations, and Togos on the vintage resale market. Bonded and faux leather sofas have a shorter lifespan (roughly three to seven years). Read on for more tips and pointers on proper leather care.

Dust your leather couch with a dry cloth regularly. You’ll also want to clean any spills or messes up immediately to prevent liquids or oils from sinking into the material. Just like our own skin, you should keep leather moisturized to maintain its vitalized appearance. Invest in a nice leather conditioner and give your furniture a good rub down once or twice a year to maintain its suppleness and prevent cracks, stains, and any other imperfections from worsening. For darker colored hides, keeping your sofa out of direct sunlight will also help resist fading. Read our explainer on how to clean leather furniture if you want to learn more ways to maintain your shiny new couch.

Believe it or not, there are many categories of leather. Full grain describes the most authentic version of the material—only the animal hair has been removed from the hide and has been naturally dyed. This type of leather is a bit tough at first, but it wears into a deliciously soft material after time. Top-grain leather is a popular option, as it’s been buffed to have a softer look (and feel) from the first sit. Split grain is a less expensive construction, as the first layer of leather has been removed. Bonded leather is another one you’ll see out there; it’s made from scraps of other projects and connected with an adhesive. No matter which one you choose, you’ll get that leather look and smell—it’s a great material for all budgets.

The world of leather sofas is expansive. With most furniture, we find successful decorating is less about the actual material and more about the silhouettes, which is certainly still the case here. Beloved for its versatility and durability, leather can evoke moodier interior vibes or more streamlined setups. Darker hues like black, chocolate brown, or navy blue signal the former—our forever muse Lenny Kravitz knows this better than anyone. Lighter tans, beiges, and taupes can feel cozy, like the vintage-looking couch we saw in this Brooklyn apartment with lots of sunlight. Since it’s one of the most popular upholsteries for modern furniture, the list of available sofa styles is endless: From classic midcentury-modern shapes with vintage appeal to softer, low profile designs that can outfit modern spaces like Devin Booker’s living room. In brighter hues, leather sofas can even be true statement pieces, like the textural scene-stealer in this Parisian apartment.

#5063. Binance hackers had 8-week intrusion window before $6.3B exploit: SEAL

The cybersecurity company flagged similar multi-stage intrusion patterns behind other high-profile crypto heists, including the $388 million Bitget hack.

Updated (Nov. 6 at 05:03 p.m. UTC): This article has been corrected to clarify the timeline of the Binance hack. The article also includes a statement from a Binance spokesperson.

Nonprofit cybersecurity organization Security Alliance (SEAL) estimates that North Korea-linked hackers compromised a Binance employee’s device about eight weeks before the exchange’s $6.3 billion exploit on Oct. 29, although Binance said the broader attack campaign began in June and that the hackers did not gain access to additional wallet infrastructure devices until Oct. 20.

The hacking group compromised a Binance software engineer’s device in September through a fake recruitment scheme, wrote SEAL in a Thursday report, adding that threat actors can navigate compromised systems with a “high degree of stealth over prolonged periods of time” after initial intrusion. SEAL’s eight-week estimate refers to the September-to-October intrusion window, rather than the beginning of the broader campaign described by Binance.

Cointelegraph contacted Binance for clarification on the timeline. A spokesperson said the initial device compromises occurred in June and September, but that attackers did not gain the access needed to reach wallet operations until Oct. 20.

“As outlined in our announcement, the initial device compromises occurred in June and September, but the attackers did not gain the access needed to reach wallet operations until October 20, when they used that foothold to move laterally and obtain access to additional devices and credentials,” the spokesperson said.

SEAL identified at least four “front companies” associated with TraderTraitor’s fake recruitment campaigns in 2026, including citalab.net, citalab.com, bitfit.io and oddium.io.

Binance traced the attack campaign back to June, when a separate employee’s device was compromised after the employee downloaded a tampered Homebrew installation while attempting to install an AI coding tool. The exchange detected the incident and replaced the device, rotating related credentials. However, the attackers had already obtained information about employees working on Binance’s wallet infrastructure, according to a Tuesday X post.

On Oct. 20, attackers used the compromised computer of the engineer targeted in September and an internal AI tool to gain control of two wallet infrastructure engineers’ devices, according to Binance. They subsequently discovered a deployment permission token, which was used during the Oct. 29 exploit.

The cybersecurity organization’s analysis pointed to similar multi-stage intrusion patterns in other high-profile crypto hacks. It estimated that attackers had about six weeks of access before the $388 million Bitget hack, six weeks of access to LayerZero before the vulnerability that led to the $293 million Kelp DAO exploit and about three weeks before conducting the $24 million AFX exploit.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation attributed the attack to North Korea’s TraderTraitor, in a Saturday report. Kim Yo Jong, sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, dismissed the accusations.

#5062. Why a Hyperliquid Treasury Firm Wants to Become a US Futures Broker

Why a Hyperliquid Treasury Firm Wants to Become a US Futures Broker

Hyperliquid Strategies built its business around holding HYPE. Now it wants to help Americans trade on Hyperliquid, too.

The Nasdaq-listed treasury firm, which trades as PURR and is chaired by former Barclays CEO Bob Diamond, disclosed in its quarterly results Thursday that a subsidiary has applied to become a futures commission merchant, or FCM.

That’s the type of CFTC-registered broker that holds customer funds and handles futures orders. If approved, the unit would carry U.S. client accounts for permissioned markets built on Hyperliquid, letting Americans trade perpetual futures within a regulated framework.

Perpetual futures are derivatives with no expiration date that let traders take leveraged positions. Hyperliquid, a major onchain venue for them, still blocks U.S. users.

Its HIP-3 framework lets outside firms launch their own perpetual markets on Hyperliquid’s order book after staking HYPE. A new permissioned version, called HIP-3*, would let a regulated deployer run markets restricted to approved traders, with Hyperliquid acting as neutral infrastructure.

“We deployed $802 million this quarter and filed an application to become a futures commission merchant,” CEO David Schamis said. “Those two moves are the same bet.”

The firm held about 38.5 million HYPE at the end of September, worth roughly $3.5 billion, and more U.S. trading routed through Hyperliquid would likely mean more fees for the protocol, which uses fee revenue to buy back HYPE. An FCM would also give Hyperliquid Strategies a business of its own beyond holding tokens.

During the quarter, the firm spent $785 million buying 9.1 million HYPE and $17 million repurchasing 2.7 million of its own shares, funded largely by $911 million raised through stock sales. It reported $620.6 million in net income, driven almost entirely by $803 million in unrealized gains as HYPE rose from about $65 to $91.

It won’t be alone. Kraken parent Payward has said it plans to deploy HIP-3* markets through its CFTC-regulated Bitnomial exchange, with NinjaTrader Clearing serving as the FCM.

And last week, NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange agreed to team up with Trade[XYZ] to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the U.S., sending HYPE up 13%. Trade[XYZ] already runs markets on Hyperliquid, including perpetual futures tied to the S&P 500.

Regulators must sign off on both the FCM registration and the permissioned HIP-3 markets it would serve, and the company has given no timeline.

#5059. Meghan Trainor. Zedd. Joe Jonas and Sophie Turner. This $18 Million Manse Is Collecting Celeb Owners. Who’s Next?

Meghan Trainor has listed her Encino, California, home for just under $18 million. If the songstress and her actor husband, Daryl Sabara, pass the torch to another notable name, it would continue the estate’s ongoing chain of celebrity stewards. Trainor bought the house from DJ Zedd in 2024 for about $17 million; Zedd had paid $15.2 million in 2021 when he bought it from Joe Jonas and Sophie Turner, who were the first to move into the six-bedroom, 12-bathroom manse when it was completed in 2019. They scooped it up for $14.1 million. To put it simply: The property has never been owned by someone without a Wikipedia page. As its listing description reads: “This compound is for the most discerning Buyer.” Unsurprisingly, the home hosts a swath of amenities that are as elite as its residents.

Tucked behind tall gates and hedges, the contemporary-style build, designed by Jae Omar, spans nearly 15,000 square feet. The dwelling’s main floor hosts a great room with two fireplaces, wall-to-wall pocket doors that open to the backyard, and a library nook. The eat-in kitchen is complete with a built-in bar and a sprawling center island with a live-edge wooden breakfast bar seating seven. An additional catering kitchen, an office, and an impressive playroom outfitted with a climbing wall, a slide, and a foam pit round out the main level, along with a fitness and wellness center and “perhaps the best-sounding studio in the world,” according to Zedd, who added that amenity during his ownership.

Five ensuite bedrooms are situated upstairs, as well as another fireplace-warmed living area. The primary suite boasts yet another fireplace, a wet bar, a bathroom, two spacious dressing rooms, and walls that slide open to a sizable balcony. Outdoor amenities include a pool and spa, a kitchen, a putting green, a projection screen, and a fire pit. A guesthouse with a billiards pub and a movie theater round out the compound. #5058. Solana’s Shanghai event halted over unauthorized promotion: Report

Solana’s promotional campaigns through domestic entities allegedly violated China’s crypto-related restrictions, an unidentified PBOC official told China’s Financial News.

Chinese authorities halted the Solana Accelerate event in Shanghai over alleged unauthorized promotional activities involving domestic entities, China’s state-backed Financial News reported on Friday.

The outlet cited an unidentified official from a department of the People’s Bank of China (PBOC), who said Solana had promoted its brand through domestic entities, including by placing advertisements on a building in Shanghai’s Lujiazui financial district.

The official also claimed that Solana provides technical services supporting the issuance and trading of cryptocurrencies, which he described as illegal financial activities under Chinese regulations.

It marked the second reported disruption of a Solana event in mainland China in the past two years, highlighting how the country’s restrictions extend beyond digital asset trading.  An October 2025 Solana event in Shenzhen was also cut short over venue capacity and public-safety concerns, according to an X announcement by Solana’s Chinese-speaking community.

In an Oct. 16 X post, Solana Foundation president Lily Liu said organizers postponed the Shanghai event to “comply with local requirements” after an event registration unexpectedly quadrupled, exceeding the event cap pre-registered with local authorities.

In a February notice, the PBOC and several other agencies doubled down on crypto-related restrictions and included a new ban on the unauthorized issuance of yuan-pegged stablecoins.

#5055. Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece's parliament passed a bill Thursday imposing a 10% capital-gains tax on cryptocurrency profits, marking a shift from a proposal discussed in October to legislation approved by lawmakers.

The measure also puts a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and decentralized finance, or DeFi.

The legislation’s crypto provisions sit inside a wider Finance Ministry package dealing with debt servicing, financial oversight, and government accounting, with the new crypto income-tax rules scheduled for January 1, 2027.

Individual investors with annual net crypto gains of €500 ($557) or less will be exempt. Cross that threshold, though, and the 10% tax applies to the entire gain, rather than just the amount over €500 ($557).

For example, an investor with €600 ($669) in net gains would face a €60 ($67) tax under the new rate, not €10 ($11). The rules generally calculate gains by subtracting the acquisition cost from the value received when the crypto is disposed of.

Selling Bitcoin for euros is one taxable event, but spending crypto on goods or services can also count as a disposal. Investors will need transaction records to establish what they paid for their assets and what those assets were worth when sold or spent.

Yield from staking, lending, and providing liquidity to DeFi protocols is treated differently. Greece will classify those returns as interest income and tax them at 15%, according to the Finance Ministry.

Qualifying crypto losses exceeding €500 ($557) can be carried forward for five years and offset against future crypto gains. The bill also gives investors a 12-month window to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto income without late-filing penalties or interest, provided they meet its conditions and pay within 60 days of filing.

The passage follows an October public consultation on the proposal.The draft proposed a 10% rate that was already lower than the 15% levy Greek officials had discussed in June.

Athens says exchanging one cryptocurrency for another should not itself trigger a capital-gains tax. But the wording lawmakers approved may not make that exclusion as clear as the government intended.

A review by parliament's Scientific Service flagged the bill's broad language covering disposals made "for consideration." That phrase could include token-to-token trades, the review said, even though the accompanying explanation says such swaps should not create taxable gains.

The reviewers recommended an explicit exemption and suggested clearer rules for exchanges involving stablecoins and electronic-money tokens. Those details matter for traders who rotate between assets without cashing out to fiat currency.

Greece is also preparing the administrative rules needed to put the tax into practice. A senior Finance Ministry official told Reuters that guidance on transaction transparency and tax audits would be issued in the coming weeks.

The government has not estimated how much the tax will raise. Officials say the market is difficult to measure because many Greek crypto investors use trading platforms based overseas.
